@@136831137 <p> The risk of stagflation grows as the cost of living continues to rise . <p> New Zealanders are becoming inured to economic challenges , potentially diminishing urgency for change . <p> As the Iran conflict drags on , the damage it is doing to the economy -- globally and locally -- becomes ever more serious . <p> Yet it feels like it is fading from the headlines . <p> Last week , oil prices spiked past US$122 per barrel . That 's the highest they have gonesince the conflict began . Higher , for the first time , than they went after the outbreak of the Ukraine war in 2022 . <p> It feels like hardly anyone bats an eyelid when they hear the economy is going south again . <p> I 'm not sure there was ever much confidence in the recovery talk before the war started , despite my best efforts to talk up green shoots . <p> So , is being inured a good thing or a bad thing ? <p> It turns out it 's a word with complex psychological and moral dimensions . My @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a boxer or long-distance runner , you need to become inured to getting punched in the face or the pain of extreme muscle fatigue . <p> A surgeon needs to become inured to the gory insides of the human body . <p> On balance , that 's all good stuff . It 's about becoming fit for purpose . <p> The word pops up in the English translation of Homer 's Odyssey . <p> " And what if one of the gods does wreck me out on the wine-dark sea ? I have a heart that is inured to suffering and I shall steel it to endure that too . " <p> Odysseus was a hard bastard . He 'd just been through hell in the Trojan War . He does n't care if the gods could throw their worst at him . He can take it . <p> But of course , there is a risk in becoming inured to the point that we just accept our lot . <p> In coping with adversity , we might cease to fight against it and give up on improving our lot @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ accept the misery around us . <p> A soldier who has become inured to the horrors of war is probably a good soldier , but are they still a good person ? <p> Some societies become inured to political corruption -- thankfully not this one yet . <p> Americans are inured to the hyperbole of the US President , something that allows him a dangerously free rein to enact ever more radical policies . <p> New Zealanders seem increasingly inured to disastrous weather events . <p> Is that strengthening our resolve to fight climate change , or are we giving up ? <p> Perhaps most tragically , in my lifetime , New Zealanders have become inured to extreme poverty . <p> In our cities , we barely give a second thought to walking past forlorn , homeless people . <p> On an individual level , there is clearly little we can do , but our familiarity becomes acceptance and diminishes any political urgency for resolving the issue . <p> I 'm old enough to remember how shocking and foreign homelessness was when I first visited cities like London and San @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ becoming inured is one of the great human dilemmas . <p> Meanwhile , markets have grown bored of the war , just as Pie Funds chief investment officer Mike Taylor assured me they would a month ago . <p> The current malaise is understandable , I guess . <p> We definitely ca n't afford to get stuck in a funk of gloom and inaction just because the macroeconomic conditions are bad . <p> Businesses ca n't keep waiting for some hypothetical recovery . They survived till ' 25 , waited for an economic fix in ' 26 . Good luck telling anyone it will be heaven in ' 27 ! <p> Humans are pretty good at getting used to a new status quo . <p> But it seems worth remembering that , prior to Covid , New Zealand experienced the second-longest period of economic expansion in its history . <p> Between 2009 and 2020 , the economy did not contract -- even for a single quarter . <p> Only the golden years from 1952 to the late 1960s offered a longer period of economic growth . <p> Business people are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on ... until they ca n't . <p> But even for the businesses that are surviving and doing okay there is an opportunity cost to this extended period of low growth and contraction . <p> Expansion plans are delayed , and investment in new technology goes on hold . Companies might not be cutting staff , but they are not hiring either . <p> We 'll see our unemployment rate stay stuck at about 5.4% when new data drops next week . <p> Do we have to accept that a rate above 5% is now the new normal ? <p> Last week , data for March showed Australia 's annual inflation rate hit 4.6% . That 's before much of the oil shock had flowed through the economy . <p> Things are better here , but not much . Annual inflation is 3.1% and expected to go much higher in the coming months . <p> In the past five years , the inflation rate has only been within the Reserve Bank 's target band ( 1-3% ) for four quarters . <p> The RBNZ is allowed to look through inflation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ we apply hindsight , all those medium terms are starting to add up to a very long term . <p> Should we just be more patient ? The great economist John Maynard Keynes had a good response to that way of thinking : " In the long run we 're all dead . " <p> So , when does resilience simply become acceptance ? <p> I remain hopeful that this latest economic shock will play through before the year is out . I feel like there is pent-up demand in the New Zealand economy and we could see a strong bounce if we can just get a clear runway of economic conditions . <p> But it 's important that we do n't drop our expectations about how a New Zealand economy should look ... and still could . <p> Liam Dann is business editor-at-large for the New Zealand Herald . @@136856625 <h> Farmers should be paid to use methane-busting tools - agritech leaders <p> Farmers need to be paid to start using methane-busting technology in their herds and on their land , agri-climate leaders say . <p> Their comments follow earlier warnings from industry and the Parliamentary Commissioner for the Environment that , without penalties or incentives , there are few reasons for farmers to invest in some of the tools . <p> Climate Change Minister Simon Watts said the government would work with farmers to " maximise the emissions reduction innovation underway " but would not be drawn on whether the government was looking at subsidies or other incentives . <p> Last year , the government scrapped its previous plans to put a tax on agricultural methane by 2030 and weakened the country 's 2050 methane emissions reduction target . <p> Instead , it opted for a market- and industry-led approach , with Watts saying that widespread uptake of the new mitigation tools would be " critical " . <p> The government-industry partnership AgriZeroNZ had so far invested $78 million into developing methane-inhibiting technologies such as vaccines and genetics . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ / Samuel Rillstone <p> Some , such as low-methane sheep genetic selection and effluent pond treatments , were available now , while others are still in much earlier stages of development . <p> Overall , the government has committed $400m to accelerate development and commercialisation . <p> At the annual Agriculture and Climate Change conference in Wellington last week , AgriZeroNZ chief executive Wayne McNee said some of the technologies had a commercial benefit because they also improved animal productivity . <p> However , many - It all adds up to another timely piece of crime fiction from Strawhan , drawn vividly from local events , but one that deserves to be celebrated far beyond our shores . 
@@136866903 <p> Since April this year , an increasing number of prominent Republican representatives and organisations are backing renewables . Photo : Getty Images <p> Expert opinion from University of Auckland - Waipapa Taumata Rau <p> Comment : Until very recently , the energy world has been split into two camps , those in support of green electrification and those committed to climate-changing carbon fuels . The latter has been led by the US and its Trump Republicans . But an increasing number of key Republicans are changing camps , motivated by the exceptional rise of solar , wind and utility batteries as the cheapest source of energy on the planet . <p> This trend in political support is also apparent in the adoption of renewables to produce electricity in the US , where they are about to surpass gas for the first time . Furthermore , the pipeline of new power coming into the US in 2026 is overwhelmingly ( 93 percent ) green , from solar , wind and utility batteries . <p> President Donald Trump 's recent attacks on renewables are n't likely to have much impact globally @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ only about 11.69 percent of global total emissions . New Zealand emits only 0.17 percent of global carbon emissions but , on a per capita basis , we emit a similar quantity of greenhouse gases to the US , driven by the production of agricultural methane . <h> read more <p> The US political system is not as negative about the switch from fossil fuels to electrification as its leader would like to convey . His attempts to expand fossil fuels and to weaken the drive towards renewables is starting to meet opposition from among members of his party . This includes a group of conservatives motivated by the fact that utility solar and wind , coupled with a grid battery , are now much cheaper than the cheapest of fossil fuels , and getting cheaper with time . <p> Since April this year , an increasing number of prominent Republican representatives and organisations have backed renewables . House Republicans have launched a push to reinstate clean energy tax credits ( GOP Clean Energy Push ) ; several Republican governors have inserted integrated renewables into state agendas to lower bills @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ offers support for solar and wind . Why would n't they ? Over 90 percent of new renewables projects are cheaper than fossil-fuel alternatives . <p> New Zealand , with its 5.5 million people , is 82 percent renewable and with 11 GW capacity , so resembling Iowa ( population of 3.2 million ) or South Dakota ( population 0.9 million ) . <p> Research has shown that factors influencing climate denialism in the US were party loyalty , alignment , and conformism . A survey reported in The Hill , showed that when Republican Party members were asked , if party leaders including Trump expressed concern about climate change , would this influence their own position , most said it would . <p> The political system in the US allows for states to go their way , rather than follow their presidential leader . Responsibility for US energy deployment and implementation lies with the states , while the federal government manages the national framework . Several Republican states are already committed to increasing renewables in the state grids . <p> An important renewables project led by the California Department @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ aims to integrate solar canopies over the roads which deliver energy to a roadside grid or to a local micro-grid . This renewable energy from the local grid powers the conductive rails incorporated in the roading and delivering inductive charging to electric trucks . Given that there are 78,400km of motorways in the US , once this technology matures it will likely have a major contribution to the national energy grid . The leading company in Electric Road Systems is ElectReon Wireless Ltd , which is active in the USA , Sweden , Israel and Germany . I would expect ERS technology will be taken up before long by many other countries including Australia , and hopefully New Zealand . <p> Republicans still do n't accept that climate change is real ( or say they do n't ) but do accept the growing gap between the cost of renewable and fossil fuels . And they recognise the reality that solar and wind energy is a gift from nature and does not require the costs of developing a coal mine : land purchase , complex heavy extraction equipment , the cost @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of removing and transporting the coal and the cost of insuring the overall mining process . Those costs do not include the health risks , the environmental impacts of extraction or the climate damage . <p> So , it 's unsurprising that the combination of solar , wind and utility grid batteries is now much cheaper than all fossil fuels . The global adoption of solar is three times faster than predicted by experts only a couple of years ago . <p> Solar and wind renewables are valuable , inexpensive energy technologies and not political ideologies . This realisation is finally sinking in among US conservative politicians . <p> The Republicans are , of course , following the money . While many Republicans still deny climate change as a concept , they are responding to the economics of clean power . <p> New Zealand politicians could take note . Progress may come not from political alignment but from practical economic incentives . Economics can reshape even the most entrenched political positions . 
@@136882370 <h> Share this article <p> Reminder , this is a Premium article and requires a subscription to read . <p> The winds above the Pacific are shifting , the ocean is releasing stored-up heat , and a cascade of global effects on rain , droughts and wildfires could be on its way , scientists say . Image / Niwa <p> The winds above the Pacific are shifting , the ocean is releasing stored-up heat , and a cascade of global effects on rain , droughts and wildfires could be on its way , scientists say . Image / Niwa <p> As the planet warms , past episodes of the natural weather phenomenon may no longer be a reliable guide of how the next one plays out . <p> Forecasters say a powerful El Ni ? o weather pattern could form later this year , with a chance of becoming one of thestrongest in three decades . The winds above the Pacific are shifting , the ocean is releasing stored-up heat , and a cascade of effects on rain , droughts and wildfires could be on its way . <p> The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ there 's a roughly 60% chance of an El Ni ? o developing between May and July . That 's a major reason scientists now say next year could surpass 2024 as the planet 's warmest year since modern records began in the mid-19th century . <p> El Ni ? o and La Nina are the opposite phases of a natural climate cycle that has been active in the Pacific for thousands of years . They appear at irregular intervals , and no two events are ever the same . But Earth 's steady warming from the burning of fossil fuels is also influencing the way these episodes shape the weather around the globe . <p> " We are now in a different baseline climate , " said Clara Deser , a senior scientist at the National Center for Atmospheric Research in Boulder , Colorado . That means past El Ni ? os do n't necessarily tell us how the El Ni ? os of the future will look , Deser said . <p> Life in South America has been entwined for centuries with El Ni ? o and its @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Meteorologists classify El Ni ? os as strong or weak depending on how much warmer those waters are than average . The strongest events cause a variety of effects across the continent : severe floods in southern Brazil in 1982-83 , drought in Colombia that ravaged coffee crops in 1997-98 , and below-normal rain and wildfires in the Amazon in 2015-16 . <p> Technically , the most recent El Ni ? o , in 2023-24 , was n't as strong as its predecessors . Yet its effects in some areas were more catastrophic . Scarce rainfall brought some rivers in the Amazon basin to their lowest levels in 120 years . Fires scorched the Pantanal , the world 's largest tropical wetland . Record-breaking rains in Brazil 's southernmost state , Rio Grande do Sul , displaced half a million people . <p> A major amplifying factor , scientists found , was human-induced warming . <p> The effects followed the expected pattern for El Ni ? o , but they were " much worse because of climate change , because now this impact is happening in an atmosphere that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ professor of physical oceanography at the Federal University of Santa Catarina in Brazil . <p> The extra warmth increased evaporation , which supercharged drought in the Amazon . It also intensified the rains in Brazil 's south , because warmer air can hold more moisture . <p> In North America , El Ni ? o typically delivers wetter conditions across the southern tier of the United States and warmer , drier weather up north . The very strong El Ni ? o that began in 1997 , for instance , saw weeks of drenching winter storms in California and heat records smashed in the Midwest and Northeast . Atlantic hurricane activity in the months before was below normal , another El Ni ? o result . <p> But the effects of the next two strong El Ni ? os were relatively muted in North America . One reason in 2023-24 , scientists found , was a pattern of unusual warmth in the tropical Indian and Atlantic Oceans that offset El Ni ? o 's effects . This pattern was likely to have been driven both by warming from greenhouse-gas emissions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ still trying to decipher . <p> " Stronger El Ni ? o events can only make certain outcomes more likely , but it does not ensure them , " said Michelle L'Heureux , a meteorologist who coordinates NOAA 's updates on El Ni ? o and La Nina . <p> El Ni ? o brings extra heat and dryness to South and Southeast Asia , but for China , it 's what happens next that 's often more consequential . After the very strong El Ni ? o of 1997-98 , the country experienced its worst flooding in nearly five decades . The Yangtze River and its tributaries were battered by two months of heavy rain , killing some 3000 people . <p> The reason was the quick swing in 1998 from a strong El Ni ? o to a strong La Nina , said Wenju Cai , a climate scientist at the Ocean University of China . The rapid switch caused a high-pressure system over the western Pacific to channel warm , wet air from the tropics and ram it toward China . <p> Scientists expect both strong El @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ frequent as humans continue heating the planet . That means sharp seesaws between the two could happen more often , too . <p> So far , it 's been relatively rare for a strong La Nina ( when the eastern and central Pacific is cool ) to flip to a strong El Ni ? o ( when it 's warm ) , " because it takes multiple years to warm the Pacific " , Cai said . But Earth 's overall warming might be making that more common , he and his colleagues found in a recent study . <p> In Africa , El Ni ? o tends to dry out two regions ' rainy seasons : July through September in the Sahel and November through March in southern Africa . It also often coincides with winds that blow warm , moist air across the Indian Ocean and toward East Africa , leading to floods , landslides and malaria outbreaks there . The human consequences can be enormous : the 2015-16 El Ni ? o caused crops to fail across southern Africa , with food production falling by two-thirds in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the atmosphere is worsening many deluges and droughts in Africa , whether they 're brought about by El Ni ? o or not , Cai said . " The dry extremes and wet extremes both increase , " he said . <p> El Ni ? o usually spells below-average rains for Oceania . Nearby Indonesia braces for forest fires ; its neighbours brace for smoke . Now , though , global warming is reshaping where and how warm the seas around the region become . This can modulate the amount of rain that El Nino and La Nina actually deliver , said Andr ? a Taschetto , a climate scientist at the University of New South Wales in Sydney , Australia . <p> For farmers , land managers , disaster agencies , insurance companies and more , nothing else provides such a reliable indication of how the world might look a few seasons ahead . " This is the best thing that we have , " she said .

@@136882568 <p> About a decade ago I had a ' Lambton Quay ' conversation with an environment policy advisor . For those who do not live in Wellington , LQ is the main shopping street which starts at Parliament buildings ; it also has cafes and government offices . Wandering along it , one often bumps into public servants with whom one exchanges personal and political gossip ; sometimes the official also cryptically comments on some issue which is concerning them . <p> ( Most public political comment is based on Parliament and the Beehive and ignores the vital and fundamental role of officials ' thinking . Politicians frequently botch the subtlety of the policies ; public understanding suffers . ) <p> In this case it was mentioned that we might not be putting enough effort into climate change mitigation . Decoding , I think they were saying that we were putting all our effort into reducing greenhouse gas emissions despite knowing that it would not have much impact on climate change ; New Zealand 's contribution is tiny . Given the world was not putting in enough effort , climate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ about trying mitigate its impact , by adapting infrastructure and land use to recognise the oncoming future changes. * <p> We have not done much , as the damage from recent storms reminds us . ( Perhaps we have done a little more to deal with rising sea levels . Have you noticed how engineers have been raising sea walls ? ) <p> We reacted to the storm surges as usual . Initial panic , then a lot of constructive support of one another . In the future we shall spend a fortune remedying the infrastructural damage , much of which we could have avoided by spending less earlier in readiness for the surges . <p> Too much of our policy is like that . We do not look forward , we fail to do adequate prevention , we handle the immediate crisis reasonably well , but it is painful to those directly hit , and then we spend a fortune on after-crisis repairs . <p> That is not true on all fronts , but here are a couple of further examples . The Wellington water system is crumbling . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ overloaded the storm water system . Flood water poured into the sewage system bursting a sewer pipe which flooded houses in Kilbirnie . Remedial work followed , but the pipes remained old and poor and there are further ongoing failures ( usually not of the same magnitude ) . A mere thirty years later , in 2024 , the Wellington City Council began developing a long term plan . <p> The issue is not unique to Wellington . The Auditor General reported in 2019/20 , that the amount councils -- excluding earthquake-recovering Christchurch -- spent renewing pipes and other plant was 74 per cent of depreciation for water supply , 64 per cent for wastewater and just 39 per cent for storm water . A 2025 report said that local bodies were planning o do better , but that is a hell'uve a backlog . It is projected that we shall have to spend $49b on water infrastructure over the next decade . <p> The water system is largely out-of-sight out-of-mind ; we treat roads better because they are above ground . We have been keeping rates and water charges @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ( consuming ) public ( or collective ) capital in order to increase private consumption . In the longer run that means we have poorly performing public goods ; typically by then the culprits are no longer in power . <p> My impression is that there are many other areas where we fail to maintain , or fail to provide for increasing demand , in order to increase current consumption , thereby plundering the future . Not all of them . Since the Kaikoura earthquakes , Wellington has been making a major effort to become more robust to earthquake shocks . Even so , one report estimates that 97 percent of our expenditure on natural hazards is on backward-looking remediation , 3 percent on forward-looking mitigation . <p> A different class of failures to face up to the future is illustrated by the ageing of the population . Following Treasury 's first Long Term Fiscal Projection in 2006 -- twenty years ago -- it became well recognised that the ageing population would put almost intolerable pressure on the fiscal system . The failure arises not only because of the increasing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ older population has higher demands on the healthcare system ; the older it is , the greater the pressure . <p> It is a bit like water infrastructure . We have known for some time , the fiscal pressure was coming , but we have done hardly anything about it. ** My fear is that when we hit the fiscal crisis , there will be a sharp cutback on the level of NZ Superannuation and publicly funded healthcare and other entitlements . It will be all the more distressing because the most affected will be hardest hit . <p> Unusually -- and twenty years late -- the Treasury has just gone public about its concerns . I do n't particularly agree with its policy recommendations . Mine are twofold ( as well as strengthening the Kiwisaver provisions ) . <p> First , we should implement a rise in the age of entitlement to NZS . The increase should be in incremental steps and well signalled ( recall the shift from 60 to 65 in the 1990s ) , with a backup of an ( incomes-tested ) invalids ( or ' @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ age who can not be reasonably expected to be employed or live from their savings . <p> Second , we should introduce a special tax code for NZ Superannuitants which would have the same tax rate at low and medium-low levels of income as for other taxpayers . But the rates would be higher above those income levels , reducing the value of NZS to the more affluent . ( Yes it is a surcharge -- the fiscal pressure can not be resolved without one -- but it is applied in a way which is simpler to understand . ) <p> Of course there will be an outcry , led by you-know-who , even though initially few will be affected , You can not make an omelette without breaking eggs ( that 's Rabin 's Law ) . I would blunt the outcry by recycling most of the immediate savings back to the elderly , perhaps as greater housing and local body rates assistance , improved healthcare ( say reducing waiting lists ) and residential support . The gains I am after are long term , restraining rising spending . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ more slowly than for ordinary taxpayers . ) <p> Again we have been funding current consumption -- in this case of the very well-off elderly -- and so compromising the future . Again I am arguing , as I did above for infrastructure , that we should spend now to reduce harm in the future . In the NZS restructuring case any additional spending need only be a little , although it could be dressed up to appear a lot ( such as including in the package healthcare spending which would have been already partially committed anyway ) . <p> In the end we need to think more about the future and avoid the inertia of nostalgia . The future soon turns into the present . Marlborough District ... <p> New scientific modelling is offering a more optimistic outlook for parts of Marlborough , with updated data showing a reduced threat of coastal flooding in the Lower Wairau Plain . <p> Marlborough District Council says its latest hydrodynamic modelling -- developed with Earth Sciences NZ -suggests the risk from sea level rise and coastal inundation near Blenheim is lower than previously thought . <p> The findings mark a significant shift from earlier " bathtub " models , which tended to overestimate flood risk . The new approach instead simulates how water actually behaves -- including interactions with rivers , lagoons , stopbanks and coastal barriers -- giving a more accurate picture of potential inundation . <p> Mayor Nadine Taylor says the updated modelling strengthens confidence for future planning , with a smaller area now identified as being at risk . <p> However , the council is warning the threat has not disappeared . Parts of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ relatively small rises in sea level , and risks increase significantly under higher sea-level scenarios of around 1.8 to 1.9 metres . <p> The modelling also highlights how coastal storms can push river levels higher -- reinforcing the need for joined-up management of both river systems and coastal environments . <p> Council says the improved data will help guide future decisions on land use , infrastructure investment , and climate adaptation planning , as part of its long-term strategy through to 2028 . <p> Further work is already underway , including investigations into how rising sea levels could affect groundwater across the lower plain -- an issue that could impact farming and land productivity . <p> For Radio Waatea , while the updated modelling brings some reassurance , it also underscores the ongoing challenge of climate change -- and the need for iwi , councils and communities to work together on long-term solutions . <h> Stay Updated <p> RECEIVE REGULAR UPDATES FROM WAATEA NEWS <h> Stay Updated <h> Radio Waatea is Auckland 's only Maori radio station that provides an extensive bi-lingual broadcast to its listeners . @@136914834 <h> Share this article <p> Reminder , this is a Premium article and requires a subscription to read . <p> As Taharaki Skyside by Fiona Pardington ( pictured ) marks 25 years of our participation in the Venice Biennale , Mark Amery looks at the legacy of previous exhibitors and their installations . Photo / Meek Zuiderwyk <p> Venice is sinking under her own weight . Multibillion-dollar flood defences -- steel gates attached to the seafloor -- are needed more and more . <p> Yet still we pile on , no more so than every two years between May and November for the world 's most significant contemporary art event , La Biennaledi Venezia -- the Venice Biennale . <p> The oldest biennale of now hundreds worldwide , this year the event will have 99 national pavilions , a huge central curated international exhibition , and at least 31 collateral events around the edges . <p> It 's political . No more so that this year -- a week out from this year 's event starting on May 9 , the international jury resigned due to the participation of Russia @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ until November . <p> The five member jury is responsible for choosing the winners of the golden and silver lion awards from about 110 artists participants . The panel of five curators , art historians and academics from Brazil , Australia , Spain , United States and Italy had earlier said they would not be awarding prizes to artists from countries whose leaders were facing charges at the International Criminal Court . <p> The Russian pavilion was reopening after a break , despite Kyiv and 21 EU countries protesting , and Russian activist band Pussy Riot will attempt to replace it with an exhibition of art by political prisoners . Italian Prime Minister , Giorgia Meloni said allowing Russia to take part " is a decision not shared by the government " . <p> Meanwhile , seven countries , including Qatar , Somalia , and Vietnam , are participating for the first time , adding to the grand , ornate pageant highlighting our treatment of this Earth and each other with the art trying to cut through cacophony . <p> The biennale remains a grand , ornate pageant highlighting our @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ trying to cut through cacophony . <p> New Zealand 's pavilion is produced by Creative New Zealand , but this year for the first time they have contracted Christchurch Art Gallery as an external partner to deliver the actual exhibition , following a 2022 review questioning CNZ 's capacity . <p> Fiona Pardington 's Taharaki Skyside presents large portraits of taxidermied native birds -- from red kaka kura to albino tui . The species are either extinct , endangered or vulnerable . Stuffed , yet hauntingly full of personality , they " judge us and are us " , as arts journalist Andrew Paul Wood writes in the catalogue . Appropriate for Venice : a majestic , threatened city of ghosts . <p> Pardington ( Kai Tahu , Kati Mamoe , Ngati Kahungunu ) draws on these birds ' Maori spiritual significance as messengers between the human and divine . Taharaki Skyside will be housed in Venice 's Istituto Santa Maria della Piet ? , a former orphanage for abandoned children . CNZ used it in 2013 to great scenic effect for Bill Culbert 's Front Door Out Back . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's first Venice appearance in 2001 , when our reps were fellow Ngai Tahu artists Jacqueline Fraser and Peter Robinson . Like Robinson , Pardington has been inspired by Dante 's 14th-century Divine Comedy , where spiritual messengers guide the artist through otherworldly realms , and purgatory is an island mountain in the Southern Hemisphere . First , however , Dante must go through hell . Robinson 's 2001 installation , Divine Comedy , recalled the nine concentric circles of this inferno . <p> In 2001 , three years after the Ngai Tahu Claims Settlement Act , there was also representation by Otautahi kapa haka group Pounamu Kai Tahu in the parallel Venice Dance Biennale . Performing at St Mark 's Square , they made the front pages of Italian newspapers . <p> New Zealand at Venice tends to involve artists like Fraser and Robinson and curators who have spent years building international careers . Robinson was living in Germany by the mid-1990s and appearing in other biennale . Like Fraser , he managed to turn marginalisation as a Maori in contemporary art into a strength long before it was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cusp of a significant international career . He included both artists , and Pardington as it happens , in a touring " export " show of New Zealand work in Germany and here in 1996 , cheekily titled Cultural Safety . <p> Our Venice debut was something the government and Creative NZ had to be persuaded was a good idea . Living in Italy at the time , curator Sophie Jerram recalls feeling in early talks in 1996 that Creative NZ and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Trade were " indifferent about this opportunity " . Jerram was " slipped $2000 " by arts patron and first Venice commissioner Dame Jenny Gibbs and former National Art Gallery director Luit Bieringa to help with networking in Italy . <p> If it ever was , Aotearoa is no longer purgatory for artists . New Zealand at Venice coincided with an acceleration of globalisation , with artists able to move more freely -- physically and digitally -- between hemispheres . <h> Also on show <p> Venice Biennale has often crudely been dubbed the " Art Olympics " , and in getting the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ stories about New Zealand artists overseas get ignored . For example , few at home will know Megan Tamati-Quennell ( Ngai Tahu ) was one of the co-curators of the significant 2025 Sharjah Biennial , to which she took nine artists . Or that the Sydney Biennale is on now until June , with participation from Tamaki Makaurau-based Benjamin Work and Poneke-based Iranian Kurdish journalist Behrouz Boochani . <p> Meanwhile , at the Art Gallery of New South Wales , Otepoti Dunedin-born Mike Hewson 's The Key 's Under the Mat is a sensation ( extended to August ) , featuring a barbecue , laundromat and playground . <p> Venice is more in line with world expos and fairs , something Berlin-based Michael Stevenson 's This is the Trekka spoke directly to in 2003 . Stevenson brought together New Zealand 's only designed and produced vehicle , the Trekka , with the Moniac ( a 1940s NZ designed hydraulic computer modelling the economy using water flow ) and a wall of butter boxes . Old isolationist economic theory spoke back to global neoliberalism . <h> The controversy <p> Perhaps it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Kiwi **42;785;TOOLONG controversy . The selection of anonymous collective et al . for the 2005 Biennale raised an outcry after their previous exhibition had been dubbed by an Act MP as looking like a portaloo that brayed like a donkey . <p> Yet the works sent to Venice are n't principally for New Zealand audiences ; et al . had been awarded our most prestigious art award , the Walters Prize by judge Robert Storr of New York 's Museum of Modern Art and later director of visual arts at Venice . <p> The et al . exhibit , the fundamental practice , was critically acclaimed in Venice , much visited , and now feels utterly prescient . Drained of colour , the installation was described as like a makeshift laboratory or internment camp with human-sized " autonomous purification units " erratically , crankily moving across the floor , their computer-generated voices reading extremist texts . Conflicting viewpoints not listening to each other . It was intended to destabilise , and it did . <p> The PR hitch did at least bring Biennale attention back home , but led in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Works less likely to antagonise back home , yet no less bold , followed : colourful table figures by Francis Upritchard and giant abstracts by Judy Millar in 2009 , and Michael Parekowhai 's carved grand pianos and cast bronze bulls in 2011 . Photo / Michael Hall <p> Returning to a more challenging proposition for the mainstream , Dane Mitchell 's 2019 project Post Hoc included seven " Frankenpines " -- stealth transmission towers disguised as pine trees -- placed around Venice , broadcasting a list of extinct , obsolete and withdrawn things . That year , Venice had its worst floods since 1966 and a large tree , roots and all , came down next to a Frankenpine that remained standing . <p> In 2022 , Samoan-Japanese artist Yuki Kihara @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ represent us at Venice with Paradise Camp , which spoke to climate change and tourism . <p> Creative NZ did not take a pavilion in 2024 due to inadequate funding and workload concerns , but that did n't stop it being our most successful year yet . Mataaho Collective 's giant woven installation Takapau , originally commissioned by Te Papa , won the biennale 's top prize , a Golden Lion for best participant -- a bit like Jane Campion 's The Piano winning the Palme d'Or at Cannes . <p> Participation at Venice is n't cheap , though Creative NZ has committed to 2026 , 2028 and 2030 . @@136915243 <h> Learn how to be a confident composter - it 's easier than you think <p> If you believe composting is only for people who have big gardens and green thumbs as it 's too complicated , think again . <p> Whether you rent or own , live in an apartment , a townhouse , or a home with a big garden , composting is actually straightforward , and it helps keep organic waste out of landfill and turn it into something useful . <h> Why composting matters -- for you and the planet <p> No matter how or where you live , there 's a way to compost that works with your lifestyle . <p> Apartments or units : Small-space solutions like worm farms or bokashi bins are designed for indoor or balcony use . They 're compact , low-odour and ideal if you 're short on space . Diluted worm tea or bokashi juice makes nutritious fertilisers for your plants . <p> Townhouses : A small compost bin or worm farm can fit neatly in a courtyard or narrow outdoor space . These systems are easy to manage @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ them into compost you can use in planters and pots . <p> Houses with a garden or backyard : Traditional compost bins are great for households with outdoor space . They can take larger volumes of food scraps as well as garden waste , like grass clippings , turning it into compost for lawns , veggie patches or flower beds . <p> Around half of what Aucklanders throw in the rubbish bin is food and garden waste . When this ends up in landfill , it creates methane - a powerful greenhouse gas that contributes to climate change . <p> Composting is an easy way to live more sustainably because it : <p> reduces greenhouse gas emissions <p> cuts down on rubbish going to landfill ( and gives you more room in your rubbish bin ! ) <p> makes you part of the circular economy by giving organic resources back to the whenua instead of being wasted . <p> Many people also find composting connects them more closely with what they eat , how much they waste , and the cycles of nature . It 's good for your health @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ A composting solution for every home <p> Temporary housing : There are plenty of portable composting options that do n't require digging holes or making permanent changes ( although composting improves soil wherever you do it ! ) . Worm farms and bokashi bins can move with you when you do . <h> Compost what you can at home -- food scraps collection takes care of the rest <p> Not everything can go in a home compost , but that 's where Auckland Council 's food scraps collection service comes in -- it 's designed to work alongside home composting , so that whatever you do n't put in the compost , you can put in the food scraps bin . <p> Home composting is perfect for the scraps you can easily manage , like fruit , vegetable peelings and coffee grinds . Different systems can take a range of things -- but no matter the system that suits your lifestyle , the food scraps bin takes care of the rest . Just pop any food waste that does n't go in your chosen home compost system ( such as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ food scraps bin and this excess waste gets turned into energy and fertiliser -- making the most of every scrap ! <h> Composting is easier than you think <p> One of the biggest barriers to composting is the idea that it 's complicated or messy . In fact , most systems take just a few minutes a week to manage , and if you get the right mix in your compost by adding carbon like dried leaves or paper , it 's low odour and low maintenance . <h> Learn how to compost with confidence <p> If you 're not sure where to start or if you 've tried composting before and it did n't quite work , then help is close at hand . The Compost Collective runs free workshops across Tamaki Makaurau , helping Aucklanders find the composting method that works for their home and ensuring they have the tools and knowledge to get started . <p> You 'll learn : <p> which composting system suits your home <p> what you can and ca n't compost <p> how to avoid smells and pests <p> how composting and the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ workshop realising that composting is n't an " all or nothing " commitment -- it 's about doing what works for you . <p> As a bonus , all Compost Collective composting workshop attendees receive a discount coupon towards a composting system of your choice . So what are you waiting for ? Find a workshop and sign up at the Compost Collective website and you 'll be a confident composter in no time !

@@136948816 <p> Although New Zealand has one of the world 's highest levels of residential property insurance coverage , the Reserve Bank ( RBNZ ) says rising rebuild costs , climate change and improved modelling of seismic hazards are leading to rising premiums and increasingly impacting insurance affordability and availability . <p> Releasing its latest Financial Stability Report on Wednesday , the RBNZ said insurance played a critical role in supporting financial stability in New Zealand because of the economy 's high exposure to natural hazards and the importance of residential property as a household asset and " a core component of bank collateral . " <p> While these emerging pressures are unlikely to be a risk to New Zealand 's financial stability in the short term , the report said " aggregate data may hide pockets of risk . " <p> The RBNZ estimated the total sum insured of New Zealand residential dwellings in 2024/2025 was around $1.5 trillion . <p> This money goes into the Natural Hazard Fund and is used to cover claims after a natural hazard event . The fund is also used to buy reinsurance from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ NHC Scheme and goes towards research and education . <p> For each natural hazard event , the NHC currently pays $300,000 towards rebuilding or repairing a residential home . This is called a building cover cap and currently , the Natural Hazards Insurance Levy is 16 cents per $100 of the insurance cover amount . <p> The options put forward for consultation are to maintain the levy at its current rate of 16 cents per $100 of cover , or increase the rate to either 22 cents , 24 cents or 25 cents per $100 of cover . <p> In February , NHC chairman Chris Black told the Finance and Expenditure Select Committee that the levy would have to go up at some stage . <p> The Financial Stability Report said while for many New Zealanders , these costs remained manageable , " pockets of vulnerability likely exist , particularly in higher risk areas or for those with lower incomes , such as retirees " . <p> Underinsurance <p> The report said the scale of potential underinsurance does n't currently appear large enough to pose a systemic threat . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the report said . <p> " Underinsurance could amplify losses following a major event , particularly if rebuild costs continue to rise or if inflation exceeds adjustments to sum-insured values . " <p> The report said consumer survey evidence shows people with insurance can struggle to figure out what 's an appropriate amount to insure themselves for . <p> " Underinsurance may also be a rational choice for policyholders trying to manage the overall cost . " <p> Insurance retreat <p> Only a small percentage of homes are exposed to near-term risk of losing insurance , the report said , and if insurance retreat did happen , " national insurance penetration would remain robust by international standards " . <p> " However , the distribution of retreat risk is uneven , with coastal communities exposed to sea level risk and erosion being the most affected , with inland properties facing high flood or landslide risks also at risk of increasing exclusions or non-renewal . " <p> Potential impacts on the banking sector <p> The report said previous large-claims events have had limited impacts beyond the insurance industry . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the report said several areas could end up impacting the banking sector . <p> This includes : <p> Credit risk through collateral impairment : Properties that are n't insured face depressed market values and may be unsellable which could limit banks ' recovery options <p> Reduced mortgage ability : The report said banks could choose to " red line " areas with high insurance uncertainty , creating pockets of financial exclusion and potential price declines <p> Operational and legal exposures : Mortgage contracts require borrowers to have insurance but banks lack the tools to systematically monitor this <p> Market functioning and confidence : High-profile cases of uninsured losses could erode public trust in insurers and lenders , even where aggregate risks are low <p> " Banks have begun to explore responses to these risks , " the report said . <p> " Some banks get information from individual insurers to identify under-insurance of properties in their mortgage portfolios . Some are assessing physical risks , such as flood risk , at loan origination rather than relying solely on whether a property is insured . <p> " For properties identified @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ considered such as restricting top-ups or limiting loan-to-value ratios for new lending . " <p> Your access to our unique and original content is free , and always has been . @@136949127 <p> Ted Turner , the flamboyant US entrepreneur who transformed television news with the creation of CNN in 1980 , has died at the age of 87 , the network said on Wednesday . <p> The mustached southerner , yachting enthusiast and philanthropist , whose empire also included sports clubs , had been suffering from the degenerative disease Lewy Body Dementia . <p> Cable News Network upended established broadcasting with its dedication to around-the-clock breaking news and shot to global recognition with its coverage of the Gulf War in 1990-91 . <p> The 24-hour network was the first in the United States to run non-stop news and quickly built a worldwide footprint . <p> Correspondents brought live coverage from major events ranging from the collapse of the Soviet Union to the Chinese crackdown on the Tiananmen Square protests . CNN 's decision to keep reporters in Baghdad amid US bombing on the Iraqi capital cemented the network 's reputation as an indispensable source of breaking news . <p> " Ted is the giant on whose shoulders we stand , and we will all take a moment today to recognise him @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " Mark Thompson , chairman and chief executive of CNN Worldwide , said in a statement . <h> 20th century media giant <p> Ted Turner created CNN in 1980 , revolutionising the way America , and the world , got their news , and then went on to become one of the most prominent business leaders and philanthropists of his era . <p> A brash risk taker , Turner helped reshape the television industry in the late 20th century . <p> He also made a name for himself with spectacular business deals , his ownership of professional sports clubs , a marriage to actress Jane Fonda , his leadership of a competitive yachting team and then a devotion to charitable and environmental causes . <p> Born in Cincinnati , Ohio in November 1938 , Robert Edward " Ted " Turner III went to a military boarding school in Tennessee , and then attended Brown University , but was expelled before graduating . <p> Turner took over a faltering family advertising business after his father , despondent over financial problems , committed suicide . <p> After buying a number of radio @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in 1970 was his first move into television . <p> Ten years later , that became the flagship of his nationwide Turner Broadcasting System , the profits from which he parlayed into the launch of CNN . <p> CNN launched in 1980 as the first 24-hour cable news network , gaining traction in the United States and later internationally . <p> CNN founder Ted Turner Photo : AFP / Don Emmert <p> The launch came as viewers were shifting from broadcast to cable and CNN became a key source of news during the 1990-1991 Gulf War , delivering extensive live coverage using satellite technology . <p> Turner 's television empire expanded beyond CNN and included TBS and TNT channels for sports and entertainment , Turner Classic Movies and Cartoon Network , among others . <p> As his television empire expanded , Turner made a failed bid to acquire CBS in the 1980s and then briefly acquired MGM/UA Entertainment Co. studios . <p> Turner sold the Hollywood group back months later , while keeping the rights to large portions of its catalog including films from MGM . <h> Blockbuster deals <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ merged with Time Warner in 1996 , making him vice chairman of the media-entertainment powerhouse . <p> In 2001 , AOL ( previously America Online ) completed its purchase of Time Warner for US$165 billion in the biggest merger in corporate history . <p> But the deal had to be unwound within a decade with the falling fortunes of AOL , which used an inflated share price for the deal . Turner , the biggest shareholder of Time Warner , lost billions from the deal . <h> Sports figure <p> Turner bought the Atlanta Braves baseball team in 1976 and the team made several World Series appearances , winning in 1995 . He sold his interest as part of the Time Warner deal , but the team continued to play on Turner Field until 2016 . <p> He also owned the Atlanta Hawks NBA team and Atlanta Thrashers NHL team , both of which were transferred to Time Warner as part of the 1996 megadeal . <p> In other sports endeavours , Turner successfully skippered the US yacht Courageous to win the 1977 America 's Cup . <p> A yachting @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ when a Murdoch-sponsored yacht collided with Turner 's in the Sydney-Hobart race , sinking Turner 's schooner . <p> Afterwards , Turner challenged Murdoch to a fistfight and the two media icons continued to play out their rivalry in the business world . <p> Turner created the Goodwill Games in the 1980s as part of an effort to ease international tensions during the Cold War , amid Olympic boycotts led by the United States and Soviet Union . The last events were held in 2001 . <h> Attention getter <p> Aside from his business and sports deals , Turner gained attention for his personal life and his later ventures focused on philanthropy and the environment . <p> In 1996 , he complained to The New York Times that " all the money is in the hands of these few rich people , and none of them give any away " . <p> He was Time magazine 's man of the year in 1991 , the same year he married , actress Jane Fonda , his third wife . They divorced a decade later , and he blamed their marital woes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " He needs somebody there for him 100 percent of the time ... That 's not love , it 's babysitting . " <p> In 1998 , he donated a billion dollars to the United Nations to create the UN Foundation , which has focused on climate change , sustainable development , technology and health initiatives . <p> Turner announced his departure from his post at Time Warner in 2003 to devote his energy to his other interests . <p> These include the Turner Foundation , founded in 1990 , which supports efforts to fight climate change and improve air and water quality and has given away some US$380 million ( NZ$638m ) . <p> His Turner Endangered Species Fund , launched in June 1997 , has worked to save gopher tortoises , monarch butterflies and leopard frogs , working mainly with private landowners . <p> In 2015 , he launched the eco-tourism venture Ted Turner Reserves , which enables tourists to visit his four properties in New Mexico and learn about conservation efforts . 
@@136983009 <p> As hearings on Santana Minerals ' controversial application to build four massive open-pit mines near Bendigo in Central Otago draw to a close , focus has shifted to the Australian company 's relationship with Ka Runaka , which represents four hapu of Ngai Tahu . @@136983134 <p> Urgent , decisive action is needed on how communities will pay for the costs of adapting to climate change , a major new report says . <p> Climate-driven severe weather events were already causing " long-lasting hurt , grief and fear " , and tens of thousands more peoplewould likely be exposed to hazards by 2050 , the Climate Change Commission said . <p> However , there were " extreme " shortfalls in policy to address some of the biggest risks , including vital decisions about how to fund and guide adaptation and relocation . <p> Commission chief executive Jo Hendy said that had left the country in " react and recover " mode where too much money was spent cleaning up after events , instead of on proactive measures to limit damage and build community resilience . <p> The commission 's National Climate Change Risk Assessment , released today , identified what it said were the 10 biggest risks to the country from climate change . <p> Threats to buildings , road and rail , and water infrastructure are all on the list , but it also includes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ decision-making . <p> The country 's " degraded " water infrastructure would be at extreme risk by 2050 , hundreds of thousands of buildings were already exposed to coastal or inland flooding hazards , and the current emergency management system " lacks the capacity or capability to deal with significant , complex , widespread events impacting multiple regions at once " . <p> " Many councils lack the funding or borrowing capacity to directly implement the changes they have identified , " the report said . " This delays resilience building and increases future costs . " <p> There were " high human and financial costs when people are forced to move " , and uncertainty about a community 's future could erode people 's sense of safety and belonging . <p> The prospect of relocation might be a necessary solution in some places , but could " break relationships , divide communities and undermine trust in institutions " . <p> Well-planned and managed relocation could reduce those risks but that required " long lead times " , the commission said . " It is important to start as soon @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in Hawke 's Bay burst its banks during Cyclone Gabrielle . <p> In pressing for urgent action , the commission was aware of cost-of-living pressures and constraints on government budgets , Hendy said . <p> " The point is that we 're actually already paying ... every time we react . " <p> The choice was not between funding climate resilience or paying for other things the country needed , she said . <p> " The choice is whether we stay paying to clean up the same disruption over and over again , or we move to actually put that money into building resilience . " <p> Climate Change Minister Simon Watts has said that decisions about cost-sharing will not be made until the next term of government . <p> An expert working group commissioned by the previous Government published a lengthy report in August 2023 , that set out how planned relocation could take place , including suggested levels of compensation . <p> However , its report came too late to be picked up by the previous Government . <p> In 2025 , an independent reference group commissioned by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ councils . " <p> Hendy said there was now " much more lived experience " of climate-related extreme weather . <p> " People are experiencing increasing disruption from storms and floods right now , and that 's really ramped up . " <p> The latest climate projections showed that weather extremes of all kinds would continue to increase in intensity and frequency throughout this century , the report said . <p> " This includes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ result ) , very hot days and high winds , drought and wildfires , and sea-level rise and coastal inundation ( flooding ) . " <p> The number of people exposed to coastal flooding could rise from 32,000 to about 50,000 by 2050 , and 94,000 by 2090 if little was done to limit global warming . <p> The rainiest days are projected to be 5% wetter by 2050 , and up to 10% wetter by 2090 . That would increase the risk of inland flooding and landslips , affecting thousands more people , buildings and pieces of infrastructure . <p> On top of that , most buildings in New Zealand had not been designed with higher temperatures in mind . " Under future climate conditions , this could make them at times unliveable , posing acute health risks . " <p> Poorer households would find it hard to strengthen their homes , voluntarily relocate or afford higher insurance costs . " Insurance retreat appears to have already started for some properties at high risk . " <p> The National Adaptation Framework sent important signals , but many measures were @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ action . <p> Road and rail networks <p> A quarter of roads and a third of rail lines are exposed to surface , coastal and river flooding -- putting them at risk of both short-term disruption and long-lasting damage , the commission said . <p> " Climate change is expected to reduce the reliability and service levels of road and rail networks in a variety of ways , from more frequent closures , delays , and speed restrictions to higher maintenance and repair costs , and more frequent emergency works , " it said . <p> " The consequences are especially severe for rural and isolated areas , where alternative routes are limited and sometimes non-existent . " <p> This was one of the most significant risks " because of the high human and financial costs when people are forced to move , and when climate-related distress , grief , discontent and uncertainty go unchecked , " the commission said . <p> Uncertainty about housing and livelihoods could erode people 's sense of safety and belonging . The prospect of relocation might be a necessary solution , but it " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ institutions " . <p> Planning and managing relocation well , and working together with the affected communities , could help reduce those effects . " <p> Emergency management <p> Strong emergency management will save lives and livelihoods , the report said . However , the current system " lacks the capacity or capability to deal with significant , complex , widespread events impacting multiple regions at once " . <p> The Government had introduced an Emergency Management Bill in December 2025 and an Emergency Management System Improvement Plan , it noted . Photo / Sylvie Whinray <p> Nga mea hirahira o te ao Maori -- risks in the Maori world <p> For Maori , climate change was not just a physical or economic problem , the report said . <p> Many sites of cultural significance were now highly exposed to climate hazards , while extreme weather and more gradual environmental changes were affecting taonga species , habitats , and harvesting practices . <p> Climate change was also compounding structural inequalities -- many at-risk locations had higher Maori populations , and the incidence of heat-affected health conditions like respiratory and cardiovascular disease was higher . <p> Ecosystems and biodiversity <p> " Increasing land and marine temperatures change the environmental conditions species live in , while extreme weather events and wildfire cause shocks to ecosystems , " the report said . <p> Under more severe scenarios , the combined effects of climate change and existing pressures could , within decades , " push some systems past a point where they @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " to New Zealand 's current plan to reach net zero emissions . They could also affect government revenue because of their wider economic effects . At a local level , many councils had constrained budgets or had reached their debt limits . <p> Since 2010 , 97% of Government expenditure on natural hazards had been on responding to and recovering from disasters , with just 3% spent @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The Government 's National Adaptation Framework signalled that costs would be " shared across society and over time " , the commission said . <p> " While helpful for the Government to signal this , the National Adaptation Framework does not include detail of when or how decisions will be made around how costs will be shared . " <p> Decision-making and delivery <p> " The demands of climate change are putting Aotearoa New Zealand 's ability to plan , decide and act together under increasing pressure , " the report warned . <p> Decision-makers needed to " drive forward " on adapting to climate change . " Delays leave the country facing spiralling costs -- including for central and local government -- without effective ways of planning and acting together . Decisive action is needed now . " <p> The consequences of failing to manage the overall climate response would land hardest on people who were already the most exposed , the commission said . <p> " This can be the people who live in areas that get hammered by the weather events that are becoming more frequent and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ councils with lower rates income . Or it can be population groups where the impacts are disproportionate , such as for iwi/Maori . " <p> Climate Change Minister Simon Watts launched the Government 's National Adaptation Framework last year but says decisions about cost-sharing will be made in the next term of government . Photo / Mark Papalii , RNZ <h> ' Ones to watch ' <p> As well as the 10 most significant risks , the commission also highlighted agriculture and horticulture as " ones to watch " . <p> " These risks were rated at minor severity at present , but they are expected to move to major by 2050 , " the assessment said . <p> " This step change is anticipated because drought and extreme weather events are expected to affect both horticultural crop yields and feed supplies for livestock , the impacts from soil erosion and coastal inundation on the pastoral sector may become irreversible , increased temperature extremes and pest pressure could substantially affect yields , and the increased frequency of extreme events will shorten recovery periods in both sectors .
