@@136998601 <p> Cyclone Gabrielle was devastating -- and the response required meant proactive work on adaptation and emergency management was further delayed . Photo : Getty Images <p> Comment : The just-released National Climate Change Risk Assessment should send a shiver down politicians ' spines and spur them , finally , to act . <p> The major report , drafted up every six years , finds New Zealand faces a wide array of risks from climate change to our roads and buildings , natural ecosystems and bioeconomy ( including agriculture and forestry ) and even to our social cohesion and ability to fund basic services like health and education . <p> Worse , we are ill-prepared to reduce our exposure and vulnerability to these risks . While there is some work underway in some areas , the independent Climate Change Commission repeatedly finds progress to be insufficient and siloed. <h> read more <p> The one bit of good news is that our fate is not locked in . Dame Patsy Reddy , the former Governor-General and now chair of the commission , writes : " I believe that with clear , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to events to planning and adapting with confidence . This body of work gives us a shared foundation for action that will become stronger as we work across institutions , sectors and communities . If we act deliberately now , we can leave a more resilient , more stable future for the generations who will follow us . " <p> However , this will require putting an end to decades of political buck-passing by successive governments reluctant to make the tough and potentially unpopular decisions required to attain that resilient and stable future . <p> " The release of this climate risk report highlights a serious disconnect between the threats that New Zealanders are already experiencing and our highly partisan responses by political leaders , " says Dr Bronwyn Hayward , professor of political science at the University of Canterbury and an Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change author . <p> " In many ways , unfortunately this risk report could not come at a worse time . As New Zealand heads into an election we need consensus not point scoring . The hazards highlighted in the report , like flooding @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ changing climate exacerbate other risks like the cost of living or secure food , energy and water supply . These hazards do not recognise a difference between voters -- they will impact whole communities and businesses . We need cross-party consensus to make effective long term change . " <p> In theory , this should be easy to achieve . For years , political parties have pledged cross-partisan consensus would be at the root of any future decisions on adaptation . This rightly recognises that any response to the climate crisis must be durable , surviving across changes of government and the political winds . <p> In reality , however , it has been used to diffuse responsibility such that no one is accountable for actually making the tough decisions . <p> Six years ago , shortly before the last National Climate Change Risk Assessment was released , a 500-page report on reforming the Resource Management Act landed , recommending the planning law be split into two and the addition of a third new instrument , a Managed Retreat and Climate Change Adaptation Act . <p> The Labour-led government of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ was the last we heard of it . In 2022 , the two replacement RMA bills were introduced , but the adaptation bill was still stuck in policy development , the government said . <p> Early the next year , ministers were preparing to make the final policy decisions needed to authorise the drafting of the legislation -- and then Cyclone Gabrielle struck . The officials working on adaptation policy were re-tasked with focusing on cyclone recovery . The focus shifted from preparation to reaction . <p> The aftermath of Cyclone Gabrielle in 2023 . Photo : Gisborne District Council <p> Nor is this the only such case where proactive improvements have been postponed by the need to respond to a new disaster . <p> Writes the Climate Change Commission : " The ongoing strain the system is under shows in the delayed delivery of improvements in emergency management -- delays caused in part because resources had to be diverted to other , urgent disaster responses . " <p> That is part of what has led the commission to identify " risks to decision-making and delivery " as one of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Zealand . There are already major threats to the delivery of enduring adaptation governance , effective implementation of adaptation policy , the legitimacy of democratic institutions and the ability of governments to uphold Te Tiriti o Waitangi , it finds . <p> " While Aotearoa New Zealand has systems in place to guide decisions at different levels , and to help turn those decisions into action , many are not fit-for-purpose under the increasing demands of climate change . The development of new ways of planning , deciding and acting together on adaptation has begun but is not happening at the pace required , " it writes . <p> The work on an adaptation act was binned by the new Government , in favour of a much-hyped Climate Adaptation Framework which Climate Minister Simon Watts said would " set out the Government 's approach to sharing the costs of preparing New Zealand for the impacts of climate change , ... help communities and businesses understand what investment is planned in their area and also seek to improve the way we share information , so everyone can make informed decisions about @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ guide decisions before a severe weather event happens , and responses afterwards , meaning we wo n't have to start from scratch every time . " <p> Instead , what landed after more than a year of work was a trite four-page document , one of which was the glossy cover . <p> The climate commission strikes a more polite tone in its own analysis of the framework . <p> " The Government 's National Adaptation Framework published in late 2025 does not address displacement of people or communities . Neither is it clear about how action led by communities or local government can be funded , " it writes at one point . <p> And , later : " It is not clear when or how decisions will be made on a critical issue : how costs -- for adaptation planning , action , and climate-related loss -- will be shared between government , councils and private property owners . " <p> Successive failures to prepare adaptation policy are already hurting us . The commission cites research last year which found that , of the $64 billion spent by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has gone to response and recovery . Only 3 percent has proactively been spent to reduce future risks . <p> Investing early can undoubtedly save money . One case study the commission points to is the Awanui River flood protection scheme in Kaitaia -- with a budget of $15.5 million and despite not yet even being completed , it is already estimated to have averted $50 million in flood-related damages . <p> The Climate Change Risk Assessment must be a wake-up call to politicians . Making hard decisions around cost-sharing and managed retreat will be unpopular , but kicking the can down the road is an abdication of responsibility . <p> " Overall , our assessment found further work is required urgently to strengthen decision-making and delivery for an effective adaptation response across the country . This will require innovation , including to make climate change a central focus in policy and legislative reform , " the commission writes . <p> " Delays leave the country facing spiralling costs -- including for central and local government -- without effective ways of planning and acting together . Decisive action is needed now . " 
@@136998614 <p> Climate change is already putting New Zealand 's buildings and roads , natural ecosystems , bioeconomic sectors like forestry and agriculture , social cohesion and democratic decision-making at risk , according to a landmark new report . <p> Even worse , the country was doing little to prepare for most of these risks , with action in most areas deemed insufficient or containing significant gaps . <p> The National Climate Change Risk Assessment released Thursday was the first country-wide look in six years at the threats climate change poses now and in the future to New Zealand 's infrastructure , communities , environment and governance . <h> read more <p> Authored by the independent Climate Change Commission , the assessment was a sequel to the first national risk assessment published in 2020 . Under the Zero Carbon Act , a new analysis must be completed every six years , to inform a national adaptation plan . <p> The latest edition found climate change is already affecting New Zealand in myriad ways . <p> " For most of the past 10,000 years Aotearoa New Zealand 's climate has been relatively @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ variations have occurred within a relatively narrow range , " the commission wrote . <p> " This century New Zealanders are directly experiencing a changing climate . More frequent and more severe events are hitting communities all around the country , including storms and flooding , droughts and marine heatwaves . At the same time there are ongoing and progressive changes : to marine and air and land temperatures , to rainfall patterns and in the sea levels around the coast . " <h> Significant climate impacts loom <p> These patterns are expected to worsen , increasing the need for adaptation to an increasingly uncertain and unstable future climate . <p> By 2050 , the commission found , the heaviest rainfall of the year will be around 5 percent wetter . At the same time , nearly 40 percent of the country 's production land will become markedly drier . The number of people exposed to coastal flooding will rise from 32,000 today to 47,700 in 2050 . <p> For further in the future , the commission modelled two scenarios . In one , global greenhouse gas emissions are brought @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ above pre-industrial levels by the end of the century as required by the Paris Agreement . In the other , temperatures continue to rise , exceeding 3C by 2100 . <p> In the low-emissions scenario , many of the impacts of climate change are somewhat muted . Inland flooding currently threatens 590,000 buildings with a collective value of $250 billion -- in the 2C scenario this would rise slightly , with 601,000 buildings worth $255 billion at risk . <h> Change in annual number of hot days ( above 25C ) <p> Sea levels will rise 20cm above the 1995-2014 baseline by 2050 and another 20cm above that by 2090 under the low-emissions scenario . Likewise , the number of people at risk of coastal flooding would rise further , from 47,700 in 2050 to 68,100 four decades later . <p> It is in the high-emissions scenario that the worst climate impacts are felt . The heaviest rainfall will be 10 percent wetter in 2090 under 3C or more of warming . Across the south of the South Island , extreme rainfall could be up to 25 percent more intense @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ people could be exposed to inland flooding and nearly three times as many people threatened by coastal flooding in 2090 as are at risk today . Currently , 14 percent of the roading network and 7 percent of power pylons are exposed to rainfall-induced landslides . Under the high-emissions scenario , this rises to 21 percent and 12 percent , respectively . <p> By 2090 , almost all of New Zealand 's production land ( 87 percent ) will become markedly drier , with nearly half losing at least 100 litres of water per square metre of land . Almost 1.5 million people will experience up to 10 more days above 30C each year and another 326,000 would see between 11 and 20 additional days at those scorching temperatures . <h> Change in potential evapotranspiration deficit ( a drought measure ) <p> At the same time , sea-level rise would accelerate , reaching 60cm above the 1995-2014 baseline ( compared to 40cm in the low-emissions scenario ) . Researchers previously estimated that 30cm of sea-level rise would turn a one-in-100 year king tide into an annual event in Wellington and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ value of buildings exposed to coastal flooding would rise from $11 billion today to $36 billion by the end of the century . <p> On this basis , the commission said reducing greenhouse gas pollution was " integral to climate risk management " , as the higher-emissions scenario is so much more threatening to New Zealand . <p> " Higher global warming levels drive more severe and widespread hazards , reduce the feasibility and cost-effectiveness of adaptation options , and increase residual risks and losses . Continued support for and contributions to global efforts to reduce emissions and limit warming shapes this country 's future hazard environment -- it would help keep risks manageable and avoid reaching adaptation limits for communities and sectors , " it found . <h> NZ unprepared for future risks <p> What does this actually mean for New Zealand ? The latest assessment examined 37 elements put at risk by climate change , ranging from waste management infrastructure to coastal ecosystems to the Government 's ability to uphold Te Tiriti o Waitangi . <p> Already , seven of these elements were under " major " threat @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ loss and damage , serious or long-term disturbance to people , places and ways of life , and disruption to whole systems " . Another 22 were at moderate risk while the remaining eight were only minor . <p> By 2050 , without further action , 28 elements will be at major risk , seven at moderate , one at minor and one -- water infrastructure -- at " extreme " risk , defined as " there could be very large , very frequent loss and damage , permanent effects on people and the environment , and failure of systems . At this level , there is a high chance of reaching thresholds , when changes can not be reversed . " <p> Under the low-emissions scenario , 12 elements will face extreme risk by 2090 , 23 will be at major risk and the remaining two at moderate risk . Under the high-emissions projections , all but eight will face extreme risk in just over 60 years ' time . <p> In addition to examining the risks posed by climate change , the commission looked at the work already @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ It found existing policy coverage and readiness to implement was severely lacking , with overall readiness ranked " insufficient " ( the lowest level ) in 12 areas . Another 20 had " significant gaps " in policy readiness , four had " moderate gaps " and one -- stability of the financial system -- ranked best with " no significant gaps " . <h> Top 10 risks <p> In introducing the report , commission chair and former Governor-General Dame Patsy Reddy wrote that New Zealand ca n't focus everywhere at once . <p> " Climate change makes decisions more complex , but it also sharpens priorities . This is a moment for leadership -- where clear sequencing and sustained focus matter more than attempting to do everything at once , " she wrote . <p> The assessment therefore outlined in greatest detail the 10 most significant risks facing New Zealand . <p> These risks span a wide range of areas , from infrastructure to governance to the natural environment . They " are already seriously affecting people , or will soon , and will take time to prepare for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have the biggest influence on many other risks . " <p> In the infrastructure space , the three top risks were to water infrastructure , buildings and roads and rail . <p> Already , water infrastructure is under pressure , the commission said , and this will only worsen with climate change . Drinking water supplies could be contaminated or dry up and the wastewater and stormwater networks could fail , leading to overflows or full withdrawal of services . <p> Moreover , while the sector is being reformed right now , there is little to no focus on adaptation embedded in these reforms . This raises the risk of " locking in " outcomes that worsen exposure to climate change . <p> In buildings , climate-induced flooding and landslides threaten homes , schools , hospitals and businesses . High-level policies like the emissions reduction plan note the importance of buildings but this is not yet translating into practical action or change , the commission found . <p> The vulnerability of road and rail networks to climate change has been underscored by recent extreme weather which has seen whole communities @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the potential to overflow into other areas -- reduced transport connections threaten health and safety as well as the livelihoods of people and industries which depend on them . <p> Onepoto , Wharekahika and Te Araroa were hit badly in the late January storms . The whole of the East Coast was cut off . Photo : RNZ/ Supplied <p> The commission identified three significant risks to communities and human safety : Risks to social and community wellbeing , risks to emergency management and also nga mea hirahira o te ao Maori ( risks in the Maori world ) . <p> Mental health of those affected by extreme events is threatened , with research showing people who experienced Cyclone Gabrielle " suffered extreme and enduring mental trauma " . At the same time , social cohesion and community wellbeing are threatened by the possibility of displacement from climate events or managed retreat policy . <p> In emergency management , the commission found the system " may struggle to respond to the increasing frequency , severity and extent of disasters that can result from climate hazards " . Reforms are underway @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ they will be at improving the ability of the emergency management system to respond to the increasing frequency and scale of climate change impacts " . <p> For threats to the values and things of importance to Maori , the commission has published a companion report which examines seven elements at risk -- from damage to Maori infrastructure to loss of access to taonga species . <p> " Climate change compounds the effects of long-standing structural factors on iwi/Maori , such as land marginalisation and alienation , resulting in increased exposure to climate hazards and further constraining the adaptation options available to iwi/Maori . In turn , this is likely to create further impacts on iwi/Maori legal rights , cultural and spiritual practices , identity , language , knowledge , governance and intergenerational wellbeing , " the commission found . <p> Some progress is being made but it does n't yet match the scale of the risk , it said . <p> In nature and the bioeconomy , the commission identified risks to ecosystems and biodiversity and to forestry as the two significant risks , but noted agriculture and horticulture @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ risk today with the potential to accelerate rapidly to major risks by 2050 . <p> " Diverse and resilient ecosystems shield the country from the worst impacts of climate change and provide essential ' ecosystem services ' that protect people and places , " the commission found . Some ecosystems could be driven to complete and permanent collapse by the combined effects of pests , habitat destruction and climate change . There was very little policy currently under way to help the natural world adapt to climate change , it said . <p> On forestry , the sector is threatened by extreme weather and wildfires and has knock-on impacts on other elements . In particular , the commission identified slash threatening buildings and lives as a concern , as well as the release of carbon dioxide when forests are destroyed . <p> The damage in Tairawhiti in 2023 and Tasman this year show new measures are needed to cover forestry . Photo : Amanda Gillies <p> The final two significant risks were to central and local government funding and to decision-making and delivery . <p> Mounting climate impacts and the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ability of central government to fund basic services like health and education , the commission found . <p> " Disasters like the 2023 North Island Severe Weather Events -- which cost the Government NZ$6.65 billion -- are hard to budget for because their exact timing , location , and scale can not be predicted . Yet they will become more frequent and extreme as climate change intensifies , " it said . <p> In addition , the series of technically complex , politically controversial and morally fraught decisions that must be made by politicians as part of the adaptation process could continue to be delayed . This would " leave the country facing spiralling costs -- including for central and local government -- without effective ways of planning and acting together , " the commission found . <h> Join the Conversation <p> As someone who has been involved with climate activism for many years , I find it increasingly hard to be optimistic for our future . The principal barriers to effective action are not structural , technological or even political -- they are psychological : apathy , selfishness and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ politicians will do nothing . Every time I have written or spoken to ministers or MPs , their responses invariably fail to acknowledge the seriousness of what we are up against -- every reply I 've had is merely a brief and dismissive litany of weaselly platitudes . Most voters prefer not to think too far ahead , and the affluent among us seem unwilling to put themselves out by joining the fight for our kids ' future -- especially if that might mean demanding a meeting with their MP , or cutting their own household GHG footprint by e.g. ditching holiday flying or cutting meat consumption . Is there an answer to this impasse ? If so , I do n't know what it is. 
@@137017263 <h> Why not start today ? <p> No matter how much politicians prevaricate , stick their heads in the sand and try to minimise the problem , climate change is not going away . <p> It 's like that niggling toothache you need to do something about . You ignore it , but the longer you leave it , the worse it gets and the more it costs to fix . <p> On Thursday , the Climate Change Commission issued a good , hard , kick in the pants for this and future governments detailing how a warming climate -- and a rising number of severe weather events -- will affect all aspects of our society , environment and economy . <p> It 's a grim picture that 's painted . And while we say ' ' will affect ' ' , we all know climate change is already affecting life in New Zealand , despite the best efforts of the current administration to diminish efforts to mitigate against it . <p> Climate change is not going away . PHOTO : GETTY IMAGES <p> This government is making significant changes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ around emissions , curtailed progress reports and seriously undermined the work of the commission . <p> The move to cut methane emissions targets for 2050 from a 24% to 47% decrease from 2017 levels to a 14% to 24% reduction was made purely to support farmers and economic growth , despite some in the agricultural sector still complaining that the Paris Agreement on keeping global temperature rise this century to 2 ? C or lower is killing them . <p> New Zealand is now on the wrong track to be able to cope with extreme storms which are arriving more frequently . A couple of weeks ago , IAG New Zealand chief executive Phil Gibson made the startling statement that the country had experienced a damaging storm approximately every eight days during the past year . <p> On releasing the 2026 National Climate Change Risk Assessment , commission chief executive Jo Hendy said that , 15 years ago , these kinds of storms happened about once a month on average , but now it was once a week . <p> She also said the national policy settings were off course @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ natural hazards but just 3% on building resilience -- in other words , an overwhelming amount spent on reacting to events rather than preparing for them . <p> If we want a concrete example of that imbalance and the scale of the crisis , we need only think of the repeated torrential rain bringing frequent flooding along parts of the North Island 's east coast , and of Prime Minister Christopher Luxon helicoptering in with fried chicken for the locals . <p> Eventually it will not just be beleaguered parts of the country that are affected by recurring climate change disasters but all of us , if not as directly but through a lack of money for national needs such as education and health care . This assessment states that clearly . <p> It says communities are already doing hard work locally , even if that is not occurring at the national level , which is essential if we want to ultimately lower the impact and cost of future storms . Tens of thousands more New Zealanders will find themselves vulnerable to climate-change hazards by 2050 . <p> At least @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , let alone coastal inundation . It reiterates acting sooner rather than later is the best approach and that reducing global emissions is crucial to give us all more time to adapt and mitigate . <p> The government now has two years to respond with a new national adaptation plan addressing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the Budget this year for climate-change action . <p> A bucket of KFC and crossing fingers that the local marae is looking after folk just wo n't cut it .

@@137073110 <p> Major research to test whether lowering the ocean 's acidity could help to fight climate change will get underway in Hawke 's Bay on Tuesday . <p> Over the next three weeks , New Zealand and Canadian researchers will use a small fleet of boats and watercraft to sample and map the chemistry of coastal waters in the region , especially around river mouths . <p> The voyage is part of a five-year , $11 million Endeavour Fund project , led by Earth Sciences New Zealand ( ESNZ ) , to research the potential of several marine carbon dioxide removal ( mCDR ) techniques . <p> The ocean is already a massive natural carbon sink , but mCDR aims to draw extra carbon dioxide out of the rapidly warming atmosphere and lock more of it away in the deep ocean . <p> ESNZ marine biogeochemist Cliff Law said that was partly because of how difficult it was to prove that any of them worked . <p> " When things spread in the ocean , it 's very difficult to actually have instruments in place to monitor it , because @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ disperses quite randomly . " <p> There are also concerns about what effects marine carbon dioxide removal might have on the marine environment , which have driven a growing body of international law to restrict how the techniques are researched and deployed . <p> RNZ reported earlier this year on the international start-up Gigablue , which has attracted scepticism from some marine science experts over plans to carry out its own type of mCDR in New Zealand waters . <p> Instead of deliberately deploying any mCDR techniques , the ESNZ research would instead study their naturally-occurring equivalents , Law said . <p> Hawke 's Bay was the proving ground for the first of three processes , called ocean alkalinity . <p> " Alkalinity has been going into the oceans for millions and millions of years through things like rivers and from sediments , " he said . <p> " It provides a natural mechanism by which it offsets the acidity of the water . So in other words , it raises the pH and it absorbs the carbon dioxide , and it converts that into a dissolved form , which @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be exchanged with the atmosphere . " <p> Sediments plumes from floodwaters after Cyclone Gabrielle flow from rivers into the ocean in Hawke 's Bay in 2023 Photo : Copernicus Sentinel data <p> Rather than deliberately adding alkalinity to the ocean , the team would test how much carbon dioxide was being taken up as a result of Hawke 's Bay 's many rivers disgorging alkaline sediments and groundwater into the ocean . <p> " The reason why we looked in this region first of all was that we knew that there were limestone catchments and they tend to release more alkalinity into the fresh water , " Law said . <p> Canadian scientists would use ESNZ 's launch , Kimiora , to set a moored buoy with sensors , and would also operate an unmanned surface craft around the plume of water entering the bay from the Esk River . <p> " It 'll be mapping the surface waters and making measurements of ... the carbon dioxide and the pH in the water , and from that , we can calculate the alkalinity . " <p> " We will @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ things that will be indicators of the river input in the surface water . But we 'll also be making measurements throughout the water column . " <p> The team also planned to use an autonomous ' glider ' craft that would move independently around the bay , collecting further measurements , including from the seafloor . <p> That would help the researchers to measure the effects of increased alkalinity on the marine environment , he said . <p> " If alkalinity has increased , what effect does it have on things like the phytoplankton and the sediments on the seafloor ? " <p> ESNZ had already developed a good model of how river water and the alkalinity it carried mixed with the ocean , he said . <p> " The information we 'll get on this voyage will allow us to use the observations ... to actually develop our measurements and our models . " <p> Measurements will also be collected by an autonomous ' ocean glider ' . Photo : NIWA-Nippon Foundation TESMaP <p> Later stages of the research would study natural equivalents for ocean fertilisation - when @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of carbon-absorbing phytoplankton - and how much extra carbon can be stored if wood is deposited on the seafloor . <p> Rather than deliberate ocean fertilisation , the team would study what happened during a natural algal bloom , Law said . <p> " We 'll have a voyage in coming years down there to measure one of these phytoplankton blooms and measure the amount of carbon that 's falling out below it - how much is actually sequestered away in the deep ocean and where it goes . " <p> To study the effect of wood deposits , the team would look at the forestry slash that ended up on the seafloor in Hawke 's Bay because of Cyclone Gabrielle . <p> " We can look at how much of the carbon is still there and how much has been lost and how it 's impacted the biological communities in the sediment around it . " <p> Unlike the other two techniques , ocean alkalinity was a chemical process , making it slightly easier to monitor and measure , Law said . <p> " The real trouble with a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ carbon , what its fate actually is , how much of it is going to get down into the deep ocean and be sequestered away for a long time ? " <p> There were " all sorts of problems " with that . <p> " It can be broken down by feeding by animals and by bacteria , and it can be converted not only back into carbon dioxide quite quickly , but it can also be converted into other forms of carbon , which makes it difficult to monitor and measure and follow . " <p> The research project will also study phytoplankton blooms in coming years . Photo : RNZ / Cole Eastham-Farrelly <p> Alkalinity , on the other hand , was a more straightforward chemical conversion of carbon dioxide into forms like bicarbonate . <p> " We know that it 's fairly stable in those forms for long periods of time - longer than 1000 years . " <p> The full research project aimed to answer important questions about what might happen if marine carbon dioxide removal did go ahead in future , Law said . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the risks ? What are the benefits of these things ? How will they impact ecosystems and the ocean 's chemistry ? How much carbon dioxide could be removed ? How do we actually monitor and verify them ? " <p> That would help to inform New Zealand 's ministries and government " about whether this is an appropriate thing for us to be doing or not " , he said . <p> " If we were to go down this line , what do we need to know ? What regulations do we need in place before we can even consider deploying something in our waters ? "

@@137099525 <p> The concrete suggestion buried in Brown 's 800 words of general disparagement of everyone other than himself was one that raises its head from time-to-time . Outside of weird pandemic times , when voters are so grateful not to have died that they reward the governing party with an absolute majority , our MMP voting system is always going to require more than one party to agree to govern together . But rather than these agreements involving one of the major ( or legacy ) parties and some minor ( or new ) parties , why do n't Labour and National form a " grand coalition " ? A kind of NatLab collab , if you will . <p> The model for this proposal comes from the home of MMP , Germany , where that country 's political equivalents to the National and Labour parties have several times agreed to govern in tandem . Such " Gro ? e Koalition " ( or , Germany 's 2013 word of the year , " GroKo " ) have repeatedly been used to avoid having to rely on specific minor parties @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is considered necessary to prevent parties with extremist pasts or dangerous ideologies from obtaining a foothold in government . <p> You can see why Germany might be particularly sensitive to such concerns . And , while I 'm sure every reader has a view on which of our current parties is bad for the country , none have been publicly viewed as fundamentally system-threatening as Germany 's ex-communist Die Linke or far-right AfD have . So , the argument for adopting a grand coalition here in Aotearoa New Zealand must be different . <p> Replace ' cloud ' with ' cone ' and the likeness is uncanny <p> Wayne Brown , of course , thinks that because everyone else apart from him is dumb and useless , a NatLab collab is necessary to get 20 " sufficiently capable , intelligent people " running the country . Combining the top 10 MPs from National with the top 10 MPs from Labour would , he thinks , result in a Cabinet with " nearly three quarters who can do their job " . <p> An initial problem with this sort of individualised @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ intelligent people rise to the top of an organisation . I simply note that evidence does not necessarily back up this claim . More fundamentally , it reveals that Brown 's political experience is limited to the world of local government , where councils largely function as a collection of individuals only loosely grouped into tickets at election time . That very much is not the reality of central government with its much stricter forms of party discipline . <p> So , if coalitions are a matter of forming inter-party governing arrangements rather than selecting for individual talent , why might a NatLab collab be desirable ? Brown does point to one benefit . We hear constant calls for " bipartisan agreement " on a whole host of long-term problems that we collectively face , including superannuation , infrastructure and climate change mediation and adaptation . Then , whisper it , on many of these policy issues ( as well as a host of others ) , Labour and National actually are more closely aligned with each other than they are with their current potential governing partners on the left @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in government , they could craft some sort of consensus compromise that would be more likely to last for the long-term . <p> On its face , the logic is appealing . But it neglects two major factors in politics : emotion and self-interest . <p> The emotional aspect relates to how those involved in Labour and National see themselves and , importantly , how much of that self-image is crafted through contrast with the other party . After all , for some 90 years our national politics has been a matter of ( usually ) National or ( less often ) Labour governing , with the other party 's prime aim in opposition to displace them . That history breeds culture , and culture is a strong determinant of action . <p> Mates ? ( Photo : Hagen Hopkins/Getty Images ) <p> Forming a NatLab collab would involve a major reorientation within a group of people that all have cut their political teeth on stories of conflict with , and triumph over , the other . Within that political culture , it 's hard to gain traction for arguments @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ country . <p> The self-interest point depends on predictions regarding how a NatLab collab would be viewed . Brown paints a rosy picture of it having " majority voter support " that " would guarantee long terms for the MPs involved " . Possibly that is true , and given current polling such an arrangement after the 2026 election would include some 65% of MPs . <p> However , any NatLab collab would not create a truly equal union . Whichever party got their person into the role of prime minister would be seen as the " main " governing partner . To them would go the spoils of any success that the coalition achievedin terms of public support , while the " second fiddle " party would risk being overshadowed for the entire governing term . <p> Then there is the fact that those minor parties outside the NatLab collab would be free to critique the governing arrangement from both the left and the right , bleeding support from each of the parties in power and strengthening their own political positions at the next election . <p> So , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ which they try to put aside past enmities and jointly seek fixes to the country 's problems , with one of them being seen as working " under " the other while each are attacked from their right and left flanks respectively for every policy compromise that they make ? At the end of the term , do they find there is not enough support for them to form another NatLab collab , but instead they must try to make a new governing arrangement with invigorated minor parties eager to pull the country to the right or left ? <p> Or , do they choose an arrangement with their existing governing partners that allows them to be the major partner in government , advance more of their preferred policy positions , and gives their MPs the majority of ministerial positions ? @@137099888 <p> The cows were fed silage and some grain and their methane emissions were measured in a machine . <p> A hypothesis in a major dairy research programme that bulls genetically proven to be low methane producers could pass this trait onto their lactating daughters has been proven to be incorrect . <p> This was revealed by Dr Richard Spelman , the chief scientist at LIC , speaking at the recent Agricultural Climate Change conference in Wellington - an event which attracted some of the world 's leading scientists and policymakers in the field of climate change . <h> How the Dairy Methane Study Was Conducted <p> The programme began five years ago when LIC along with CRV and the Ag Emissions Centre set about identifying low methane bulls . <p> The theory was that this trait could be passed on to their daughters , which could prove to be a major breakthrough . <p> The aim of the trial , says Spelman , was to produce a cow with low amounts of gross methane emissions relative to the kilograms of dry matter it ate . <p> Two groups of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that those with high emissions - and they were mated to generate about 400 heifers . <p> " Of their progeny about 200 females were found to be low methane emitting and the other half high emitters . The final test was to see if these low emission traits remained when the cows were lactating , " he says . <h> Lactation Stage Reveals Key Limitation <p> Spelman says when they initially measured these animals at 10-12 months of age , the traits of low emissions clearly showed up and that was very encouraging . <p> But the final test was to see if these traits still existed when the cows started to lactate . <p> He says the test was very expensive - about $4,000-5,000 per animal - and complex to measure . <p> All the cows were housed in a barn because that is the only way to accurately measure feed intake relative to the emissions produced , he says . <p> The cows were fed silage and some grain . At the same time , the cows ' methane emissions were measured in a machine where the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ day through feed being placed within it . <p> LIC chief scientist Richard Spelman . <p> " To measure feed intake , you need to have animals inside and for us that is a key thing because if they were on pasture , you could find animals that produced less methane but that may be because they did n't eat much , " he says . <p> For the science team this was the crunch test because if the hypothesis was proved , it would speed up the process of producing low methane emitting cows which would have a significant benefit to farmers and the country as a whole . <p> Sadly , Spelman says , this was n't to be . <p> " From our work it does n't appear that the trait we measured in young bulls is the same trait when you measure it in a lactating cow . <p> " The traits in the cows is obviously quite physiologically different to those in the bulls , " he says . <h> What Now ? <p> While all those involved in this research programme are disappointed , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ why the low emission trait in a sire is not expressed in a lactating cow . <p> Dr Richard Spelman says it 's widely accepted that about 20% of the emissions in a lactating cow are influenced by genetics . <p> He hopes the work can continue but points out that the present trial was relatively small , although statistically valid . <p> He says in future the goal will be to measure thousands more animals in a bid to develop DNA-based tools to identify the low emitting cows and sires . <p> He says they need to build up a large data set so that at some point they can confidently go to farmers and sell the sires that will produce low emitting daughters . <p> " What all this proves is that genetics is a long game and now maybe a longer game than we thought , " says Spelman . <p> Livestock Improvement Corporation ( LIC ) and the Ag Emissions Centre have completed the latest phase of a mult-year methane research project , providing important insight into the role genetics may play in reducing gross emissions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with LIC , giving farmers a more connected view of animal performance across the season and turning routine data capture into actionable farm intelligence . <p> ACT MP and Minister for Biosecurity Andrew Hoggard says he 's hearing a common story about school buses , with empty seats , driving past pick-up points , while a parent follows behind in a farm ute , burning fuel and taking up time to get their children to school . <p> The Envrionmental Protection Authority ( EPA ) has welcomed the deicsion by the Environmental Law Initiative ( ELI ) to withdraw its appeal of the High Court 's decision confirming the Authority had acted lawfully when deciding not to reassess glyphosate.

@@137132582 <h> New Zealand 's latest climate change risk assessment , explained <p> The Climate Change Commission has scoped 37 ways climate change could screw things up in New Zealand , and compiled a top 10 to-do list for the government . <p> New Zealand 's " normal " is changing . Extreme weather events are becoming more intense and frequent -- rainfall , floods , landslides , drought and heatwaves are all ramping up as humans pump out more planet-heating greenhouse gases . <p> The National Climate Change Risk Assessment , released last week by the Climate Change Commission , paints a stark picture of what this could mean for daily life . It 's also a map , ready to guide us out of an expensive cycle of response and recovery . <h> What is this assessment ? <p> Every six years , the commission is required by law to gather up everything we know about the risks climate change poses to life in New Zealand -- from housing to health to how governments make decisions . <p> It 's a bit like a Spinoff ranking for all the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Zealand , crossed with a climate " warrant of fitness " for how prepared we are . This is the second risk assessment ; the first one was released in 2020 . <h> What is the Climate Change Commission and what 's their deal ? <p> He Pou a Rangi Climate Change Commission is an independent Crown organisation tasked with sussing out the evidence on climate change . It monitors New Zealand 's progress on reducing emissions ( not good ) and on adapting to climate ( also , not good ) . <p> It also provides advice to shape our national climate response -- like this risk assessment , which evaluates 37 different elements across seven domains . The commission has selected 10 of these as " priority areas " that we should probably get on to addressing , like , right now . <h> So what are the top 10 priorities ? <p> They 're risks that are already seriously affecting people , or will soon . <p> Buildings -- more than half a million buildings around the country are in flood-prone areas . <p> Road and rail @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to flooding , and fixing them repeatedly is expensive and disruptive . <p> Social and community wellbeing -- the hurt , grief and fear caused by extreme weather is growing , and the mental health toll of forced relocation looms large . <p> Emergency management -- already under acute pressure . In the past five years , 80% of the declared states of emergency were for severe weather or flooding . <p> Nga mea hirahira o te ao Maori -- things of importance in the Maori world . Not just physical and economic impacts , but identity and culture impacts too . This is a new focus area for 2026 , with a companion report by independent kaupapa Maori researchers assessing seven risks to iwi/Maori . <p> Ecosystems and biodiversity -- some species and systems may be pushed past the point of no return , with changes to water , soil and air affecting sectors that depend on the environment . <p> Forestry -- damage to forests from extreme weather not only limits their capacity to absorb carbon dioxide , but also cascades impacts downstream when slash tsunamis clobber communities @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ constrained budgets , local councils will struggle to pay for the costs of intensifying climate impacts . <p> Decision-making and delivery -- climate change makes choices more complex and puts pressure on our ability to take action together . <p> A Wellington Water graphic from earlier this year showing the the epicentre of the sewage overflow at Moa Point , marked by the pink star . The red stars are water testing locations , while the overlay shows the extent of the rahui . ( Image : Wellington Water ) <h> What about the other risks outside the top 10 ? <p> It 's not a pretty picture . Of the 37 risks , at least 29 are rated as already at least moderate for severity . And for 32 risks , the country 's overall readiness is scored as either " insufficient " or having " significant gaps " . Agriculture and horticulture are highlighted as " ones to watch " , facing minor risk right now but major risk predicted for 2050 . <h> How did they come up with this top 10 list ? <p> The commission @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of each risk as it stands today : minor , moderate , major or extreme . They also project future severity in 2050 , and in two different possible scenarios for 2090 . Then , they evaluate how well prepared we are to tackle those risks . <p> They also consider the connectedness of risks -- for example , climate impacts on buildings cascade to people 's wellbeing and the economy . The idea is to figure out where we can get the best bang for our buck by targeting areas with lots of connections . <h> So , how does compiling all this scary , risky info help us ? <p> The analysis will inform the government 's next official plan for adapting to climate impacts , which the minister of climate change must release within the next two years . With risks laid out clearly , decision-makers can choose to chart a safe passage through the coming climate upheavals . <h> Surely we 've already started climate change adaptation work ? <p> At the community level -- yes . Case studies from around the country highlight local action @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ tanks and solar panels , and growers are hand-pollinating kiwifruit since bees ca n't fly well in heavy rain . <p> At the national level -- sort of . The government published a high-level framework last year , and the commission is due to share its latest progress report on climate adaptation in August . But this just-released risk assessment already points out gaps : the 2025 framework does n't address displacement of people , nor essential details about how costs might be shared between government , councils and private property owners . These are big , gnarly questions we need to answer sooner rather than later . <h> What does this new risk assessment say we should do ? <p> The assessment does n't make specific recommendations , but does discuss a range of different work underway ( like water reform ) and ways to respond ( like building homes that can withstand weather extremes ) . <p> The assessment also spells out the need to break the pattern of reacting to crisis after crisis , and pursue a proactive approach , which would " substantially reduce future costs @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ expenditure on natural hazards has gone towards response and recovery in the aftermath of disasters . Only 3% has been for risk reduction and resilience . <p> " Damages and costs from extreme events are piling up , and so far there is little sign of a coordinated national response , " says climate scientist James Renwick . " We have a fleet of ambulances lined up at the bottom of the cliff , while at the top there is no fence , maybe just a flimsy string . " <p> The risk assessment is also clear that adaptation should be accompanied by continued efforts to reduce greenhouse gas emissions , which in turn will shape the climate impacts we experience . Limiting warming " preserves the choices available to protect people , places , and livelihoods , " writes Jo Hendy , the Climate Change Commission 's chief executive . " Supporting and contributing to global efforts to limit warming , therefore , matters for New Zealand . @@137132690 <h> Australia 's budget has a bundle of reforms in a time of ' extreme uncertainty ' , Stephen Bartos says <p> This year 's Australian federal government budget combines fiscal policy -- taxes and spending -- with a heavy focus on better regulation . <p> The budget deficit has fallen slightly from the mid-year update in December , to A$31.5 billion , but the budget remains firmly in deficit for the foreseeable future . <h> Tax reform <p> There are important reforms to capital gains tax , negative gearing and trusts . While the reforms are significant , the timing is cautious . <p> The capital gains tax discount -- which currently halves the tax on gains made from buying and later selling assets -- has made housing less affordable . Pre-budget rumours correctly predicted the time was ripe for this Howard-era policy to be reformed . <p> The discount will be abolished , replaced by an inflation adjustment for assets held for more than 12 months , with a 30% minimum tax on net capital gains . Changes will only apply to capital gains arising on or after @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> The government will also limit negative gearing for residential property to new builds . This , too , will take effect from July 1 2027 . The delayed start to the measures means revenue gains only kick in from 2028-29 . But they are large , starting at $1.35 billion , rising to $2.28 billion the year after . <p> In the long term , these changes will help make housing more affordable and the budget more sustainable . <p> When asked in his budget lockup media conference whether this was a broken promise , Treasurer Jim Chalmers said not acting would have been easy -- " easy but wrong " . Reforms will help housing access , particularly for young people . " I acknowledge this is a controversial change , " he said . <h> A raft of changes <p> Even larger in budget impact , but more delayed , are changes to trusts . A minimum 30% tax on discretionary trusts is being introduced from July 1 2028 . The long transition period is for " small businesses and others that wish to restructure " . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ estates and charitable trusts . Even so the measure is estimated to raise some $4.47 billion in 2029-30 . <p> There is a raft of other measures to cut taxes , mostly small in budget impact and aimed at helping business . They include extending the instant asset write-off for small business , tax refunds on previous losses for small start-up companies , and expanded venture capital tax incentives . <p> For individuals there is a tax cut , the " Working Australians Tax Offset " , of up to $250 a year . The budget also re-announces a measure from the mid-year budget update , which allows instant tax deductions for expenses up to $1,000 for work-related expenses . <p> The fringe benefits tax deduction for electric vehicles is being reduced -- another tax change with a long phase-in period . It starts small , in fact costing the budget $10 million in the first year , but grows to improve revenue by $1.57 billion in 2029-30 . <h> Spending cuts <p> There are previously announced savings in the National Disability Insurance Scheme ( $23.9 billion compared with the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in estimated costs ) . <p> Funds are also shifted between different agencies in government . There are spending increases in portfolios such as defence and social security , cuts in others like climate change and agriculture . Much of the growth in program spending was foreshadowed before the budget , such as more funding for Medicare and responses to the Bondi attack . <h> Regulatory reform <p> Past budgets have been mostly about tax and spending . This budget also includes a sweeping package of regulatory reform . <p> Some of this is in a " productivity package " that includes abolishing nuisance tariffs , faster environmental approvals , streamlining border biosecurity , and making it easier for businesses to engage with government . <p> A risk for the federal government is many of the elements of the package rely on the states and territories -- including reform to the national electricity market , harmonising retail tenancy regulation , and simplifying building regulation . <p> While this is all highly desirable for improved productivity , history tells us states and territories can hold the Commonwealth hostage . They can @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on reforms . It is a risk . <p> The government has also released a list of 14 legislative reforms to reduce regulation on businesses and households . They are wide-ranging and diverse , from higher reporting thresholds for large companies , through reducing barriers to small bank mergers , to reducing bereavement costs for families . <p> Better regulation is closely aligned to the productivity agenda . In addition to the reforms aimed at reducing the burden of regulation the government has promised further reviews aimed at better regulation . <p> It also promises , under the heading " single national market " a set of clear and consistent rules across federal and state governments . Again , progress will depend on the cooperation of other jurisdictions , which is not guaranteed . <p> In summary , the budget is workable . The NDIS savings estimates rely on compliance working . The economic forecasts assume global @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ away ) . " <p> Goldsmith said the Government was acting to give legal clarity and certainty , and to remove the possible development of a new regime contradicting the framework Parliament has enacted to respond to climate change . <p> " Therefore , the Government will amend the Climate Change Response Act 2002 to prevent findings of liability for tort for climate change damage or harm caused by greenhouse gas emissions in both current and future proceedings before the courts , " Goldsmith said . <p> The Ministry of Justice 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ compensation for harm caused by a wrongful act . <p> The Green Party has hit out at the move , saying the Government is " loosening the leash for corporate profits at the expense of our people and our planet " . <p> ' It seems really short-sighted ' <p> The litigation that Goldsmith is referring to , involves iwi leader and activist Mike Smith , who in 2024 , was granted permission by the Supreme Court to sue companies like Fonterra , over their greenhouse gas emissions . <p> Lawyers for Climate Action co-founder and president Jenny Cooper told interest.co.nz the Government 's announcement was disappointing and a knee-jerk reaction for the Government to legislate over the top of a unanimous decision of the Supreme Court in Smith and Fonterra before the case has even gone to trial . <p> " It 's also got much wider implications so I think it 's really not well thought through . To completely cut out the possibility of any tort-related claims for climate-related harm before we have any kind of legislative framework to address that issue . " <p> Cooper said @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for the harms unanswered . " It seems really short-sighted to give this , essentially , immunity , under tort law to the people who are responsible and the companies are responsible for causing the harm when we have n't got any alternative in place " . <p> Clarity and certainty <p> Goldsmith said the response to climate change is best managed by the Government at a national level , not through " piece-meal litigation in the courts . " <p> " New Zealand already has a legal framework to manage greenhouse gas emissions set through Parliament through the Climate Change Response Act 2002 and the Emissions Trading Scheme . " <p> " It is essential to maintain the coherence of the regulatory system and to deliver consistent obligations for greenhouse gas emitters , " Goldsmith said . <p> " The courts are not the right place to resolve claims of harm from climate change , and tort law is not well-suited to respond to a problem like climate change which involves a range of complex environmental , economic and social factors . " <p> ' Parliament is the place @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Speaking to reporters following his announcement , Goldsmith said : " It 's about Parliament being clear that Parliament is the place where we set our response to climate change . We weigh up all the various factors . It 's a complicated area . " <p> " We have a regime in place and people can argue about whether that regime is good or not , and that 's a democratic process . But that is the accountability . To have a parallel , separate one , I think creates uncertainty . " <p> The law change would not change the Government 's responsibilities under the Climate Change Response Act and businesses that have obligations under the Emissions Trading Scheme will have to meet them . <p> Asked about this law amendment , Climate Change Minister Simon Watts told reporters he thought having clarity in regards to policy was helpful . <p> ' Difficult to see how this benefits New Zealand ' <p> Cooper said there were a few problems with removing the option of having tort-related claims for climate-related harm . <p> " First of all , as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's someone else 's fault , then they should be able to seek a remedy , " Cooper said . <p> " The second point is that the law should not be retrospective ... if you have already suffered harm that you have a claim against somebody for , you should be able to pursue that and the law should not come in and take that away from you when you 've already got that claims or suffered that harm . " <p> Cooper said the third point was that if someone has a case that 's already in front of the courts , Parliament should not come in and change the rules halfway through . <p> " That is a very important principle of the rule of law , which potentially , if it goes ahead , this legislative amendment infringes on . " <p> While Cooper agreed that certainty of law is important , " changing the law in this retrospective kneejerk way does not provide certainty to anybody , including businesses " . <p> Cooper said it was really difficult to see how this benefits New Zealand @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the government , councils , property and business owners millions and millions of dollars . And that 's only going to get worse . " <p> Cooper said there are difficult questions such as how we 're going to pay for these impacts . <p> " It just does n't make sense to block the potential development of tort law , which has provided a really effective mechanism for these types of issues in the past , to block that from developing when we have nothing else to replace it . " <p> " There is no legislative scheme to deal with this issue , " Cooper said . <p> Cooper said internationally , this would be regarded as a serious backward step by New Zealand . <p> " I think it would put us out of step with other countries where these kinds of claims are going ahead such as the UK , the Netherlands , Germany and a number of other countries . <p> " This is still a very new and emerging area of the law but we will go from being at the forefront to being @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to climate laws ' <p> The Green Party said this was yet another example of Prime Minister Christopher Luxon " loosening the leash for corporate profits at the expense of our people and our planet . " <p> The party 's co-leader and climate change spokesperson Chl ? e Swarbrick said " Luxon 's Government has decided to prioritise protecting big polluters ' profits in the limited parliamentary time before the election . " <p> " That tells us everything we need to know about who they work for . It 's the corporations ripping us off and destroying the ecosystems necessary for life as we know it , " she said . <p> " They 've spent two and a half years taking a wrecking ball to climate laws and , at the eleventh hour , they 're now ripping away New Zealanders ' and the courts ' ability to do what this government lacks the spine to do . @@137132879 <p> Aoraki/Mount Cook is once again being celebrated not just as one of Aotearoa 's most iconic landscapes -- but as a living classroom where science , culture , and environmental education come together beneath the country 's highest mountain . <p> Educators , conservationists , and tourism operators are highlighting the growing importance of " nature-based learning " in the Aoraki region , where students and visitors are gaining hands-on experiences in climate science , ecology , astronomy , and matauranga Maori . <p> Often described as " Nature 's Highest Classroom , " the Aoraki/Mount Cook National Park offers a unique environment where people can witness the impacts of climate change firsthand through retreating glaciers , shifting alpine ecosystems , and changing weather patterns . <p> The region is also deeply significant culturally , with Aoraki holding immense spiritual importance to Ngai Tahu as a sacred ancestor . <p> Educational programmes operating in the area combine western science with Maori knowledge systems , helping students understand both the environmental and cultural significance of the alpine landscape . <h> Stay Updated <p> RECEIVE REGULAR UPDATES FROM WAATEA NEWS <h> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ radio station that provides an extensive bi-lingual broadcast to its listeners . @@137132913 <h> -- Advertisement -- <h> Shocking abuse of power -- Greenpeace slams Govt 's climate law change . <p> The Government has announced plans to pass a law retrospectively preventing companies from being sued for the damage that their climate pollution is causing New Zealanders . Greenpeace Aotearoa is slamming the announcement as a ' shocking abuse of executive power to help corporate polluters ' . <p> The proposed law change would prevent current cases from being heard , including the landmark case due to be heard next year brought by iwi leader Mike Smith , who is suing Fonterra and six of New Zealand 's other largest polluters for their contribution to climate change . <p> Greenpeace spokesperson Gen Toop says , " This Government is trying to protect big polluting businesses from paying for the climate damage they have caused , while ordinary New Zealanders ' lives and livelihoods are threatened by repeated climate disasters . " <h> -- Advertisement -- <p> " Big polluters like Fonterra and the oil and gas industry are profiting from the climate crisis , and it is everyday people who are paying @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cost of rebuilding roads , bridges and other infrastructure after climate storms . " <p> Costly floods and storms are becoming more frequent and intense as a result of rising levels of climate pollution . This year the country has already had to declare more extreme weather related states of emergency than for the entirety of 2025 . <p> Toop says that preventing the courts from considering legitimate claims against major emitters would have a chilling effect on democracy in New Zealand , and set a dangerous precedent . <p> " This is a shocking abuse of executive power . The courts exist to hold powerful interests to account and protect the public interest . Ministers should not be rewriting the law to shut down cases they do n't like . " <p> In 2024 , the Supreme Court unanimously ruled that Smith 's case could proceed to trial , finding that major emitters may be held legally responsible for the harm caused by their emissions . <p> " It is a remarkable act of hypocrisy that a Government that says it opposes retrospective law and says it wants to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ people from suing corporations for the property damage they have caused , " says Toop . <p> " Mike Smith 's case is a groundbreaking effort to hold some of New Zealand 's biggest polluters accountable for the harm they are causing . But this Government is stepping in to protect corporate profits at the expense of people , nature and future generations . <p> " We all want our kids and grandkids to have a safe future free from climate disruption . People will continue to stand up and fight for that , no matter what this Coalition Government does . " <p> Greenpeace is calling on the Government to abandon the proposed amendment bill and allow the courts to hear Mike Smith 's case . 
@@137132952 <h> Scientists Say Unproductive Land Could Help Solve NZ Fuel Insecurity <p> Could New Zealand use its unproductive land to help solve possible fuel insecurity in New Zealand ? Scientists say yes . <p> Paul Bennett , the Bioeconomy Science Institute Maiangi Taio 's integrated bioenergy portfolio lead , says New Zealand has up to three million hectares of unproductive land , including a million hectares of growing scrub . <p> He says that land could be converted to short-rotation forestry , providing a larger long-term supply and support demand for coal , gas and marine fuel replacements . <p> " Woody biomass growth can therefore be achieved without displacing food or timber or pulp production , " he adds . " This can be part of the solution for NZ 's energy security . " <p> According to Bennett , New Zealand could access approximately seven million green tonnes of low-grade wood for bioenergy production , including the sustainable and accessible biomass from forest harvesting operations , and low-grade industrial logs currently exported . <p> " This is plenty of biomass to replace the coal burnt in New Zealand @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fuels from wood , " he says . <p> Bioenergy improves energy security by producing fuel locally , meaning New Zealand would be less reliant on global suppliers , Bennett says . <p> He says there are four main reasons why the Bioeconomy Institute has been investigating bioenergy options within New Zealan -- climate change ; energy security and resilience ; market access ; and local economic development . <p> Currently , Bennett says the Bioeconomy Science Institute is looking at practical steps the country can take to reduce its energy insecurity . <p> " We are scaling up locally produced bioenergy and biofuels using existing woody biomass resources and have been working with a range of stakeholders across a range of energy sectors such as aviation , marine , processing and power generation . <p> " The current global fuel crisis has highlighted why New Zealand needs these locally produced sustainable fuel options as part of the solution to its energy insecurity . " <p> Feilding Agricultural High School is n't alone in offering agricultural or horticultural subjects as part of its curriculum , but the location of one @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fringe makes for some interesting considerations .

@@137147718 <p> Mike Smith 's children and grandchildren gathered to support him this week as news came through that his seven-year court case was to be barred by a law change , before finally getting to trial . Photo : Supplied <p> Analysis : Torts is a branch of civil law that 's developed through a lineage of many hundreds of years , allowing people to seek damages for harm or loss caused by the wrongful actions , negligence , or omissions of others . If your next door neighbour 's poorly constructed garden shed slides into your back yard , or you get gastro bug from a decomposing snail in the bottom of your ginger beer ( a real and pivotal case ! ) then the courts will likely find in your favour . <p> The Ngapuhi and Ngati Kahu people , from whom Mike Smith is descended , can also trace their whakapapa in the Far North back many hundreds of years . <p> And it is Smith 's case -- which the Supreme Court ruled could proceed to trial -- that their whenua and moana , protected by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the greenhouse gas emissions of some of NZ 's biggest companies . <h> read more <p> That 's a harm he says will be inflicted on future generations of his whanau , as sea levels rise and storms worsen . Some of those children and grandchildren gathered with him on Tuesday morning , as he got unwelcome news about a court case he 'd taken to ( as he sees it ) protect them and future generations . <p> His case -- against Fonterra , Genesis Energy , Dairy Holdings , NZ Steel , Z Energy and BT Mining -- has been working its way up through the courts since 2019 . Smith is not seeking money ; he says he simply wants a safer world . <p> " We are just seeking accountability , " he tells me . " And the payoff for us is that the reduction of emissions is going to reduce the risk for our kids and our grandchildren everywhere , not just my family . This is not a selfish thing ; we 're doing this as for the public good . " <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ should be allowed to go to trial ( set down for the High Court in April next year ) was described by legal commentators as significant . <p> It 's one of the first times in the common law world that a court has recognised an arguable possibility that tort law can be used to challenge the greenhouse emissions of a private entity . <p> But the six respondents ( there were seven but Channel Infrastructure extricated itself after shutting down the Marsden Point Refinery ) have argued that tort law is not equipped to deal with such a novel concept as " climate system damage " . That liability for climate harm should be decided by elected MPs , not judges . <p> Want to stay across stories like this ? " Our response to climate change is best managed by the government at a national level and not through piece-meal litigation in the courts ... @@137166201 <h> Share this article <p> Reminder , this is a Premium article and requires a subscription to read . <p> Parliament has moved to curb the Supreme Court 's role in climate change law . Photo / Getty Images <p> Parliament has moved to curb the Supreme Court 's role in climate change law . Photo / Getty Images <p> This week , the Government moved to reassert Parliament 's authority over the courts . Two years ago , in Smith versus Fonterra , the Supreme Court revived a climate change claim the Court of Appeal had unanimously struck out . <p> The claim included a proposed new tort of " damage to the climate system " recognised byno court anywhere in the common law world . I wrote at the time that the court had appropriated a role that belonged to Parliament . <p> On Tuesday , the Government announced that Parliament will take that role back . Justice Minister Paul Goldsmith announced that the Government will amend the Climate Change Response Act 2002 to prevent findings of tortious liability for greenhouse gas emissions . The Smith litigation , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , will be stopped where it stands . <p> Commentary on the announcement has been critical . Lawyers for Climate Action called the move " knee-jerk and premature " and said it " does not reflect the type of relationship between Parliament and courts that we would normally expect to see " . <p> This has it backwards . Smith 's proceedings did not ask the courts to apply the existing law of torts , but to invent a new claim . The Court of Appeal saw this clearly . <p> It struck out all three of Smith 's claims , observing that the magnitude of climate change " simply can not be appropriately or adequately addressed by common law court claims pursued through the courts . It is quintessentially a matter that calls for a sophisticated regulatory response at a national level supported by international co-ordination " . <p> That observation was correct and Parliament has already provided exactly such a response . The Emissions Trading Scheme is a comprehensive statutory framework covering the supply of New Zealand Units , surrender obligations and free allocation for activities exposed to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not far enough is a question for political contest , not for the courts . <p> The Supreme Court nevertheless unanimously reinstated Smith 's claims . The court acknowledged that the same claim brought against an individual driver or householder would fail . New Zealand produces 0.17% of global emissions . The contribution of any single business is vanishingly small . The court refused to apply that logic to the six defendants . <p> Causation is the spine of the law of tort . A defendant can not be liable in nuisance or negligence unless the plaintiff 's harm is meaningfully traceable to the defendant 's conduct . That requirement is not a technicality . It is what distinguishes the law of tort from a tax . When every human activity contributes to a global phenomenon in microscopic proportion , the doctrinal architecture of tort can not apply . <p> Lawyers for Climate Action suggested the Government 's proposed action undermines the " normal " relationship between Parliament and the courts . But the normal relationship is that Parliament makes the law and the courts apply it . Parliament is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The objection that Parliament should not legislate against a live case has more substance . Comity between the branches should be respected . But Parliament is not interfering with the application of existing law . It is declining to allow the courts to invent new law in territory Parliament has already occupied . Consequently , comity cuts the other way . <p> In my 2024 report for the New Zealand Initiative , Who Makes the Law ? Reining in the Supreme Court , I set out a toolkit of options for Parliament to rein in judicial overreach . The first and most direct was targeted legislation reversing aberrant decisions . The proposed climate change amendment is exactly that . <p> Those complaining about the Government 's proposal invert the traditional relationship between Parliament and the courts . Section 3 of the Senior Courts Act 2016 records , in Parliament 's own words , that nothing " in this act affects New Zealand 's continuing commitment to the rule of law and the sovereignty of Parliament " . <p> Parliament is the highest court in the land ; the Supreme Court @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ratify a course the courts wish to take , it is not acting against New Zealand 's constitutional order . It is acting as that constitutional order requires . <p> None of this weakens New Zealand 's response to climate change . The amendment removes the prospect that climate liability will be developed not by Parliament but by judges , over years of litigation . That matters to every New Zealander , not only to the six defendants . <p> Legal certainty is not a business amenity . It is the rule of law 's offer to everyone living under it -- the offer that people can read a statute and plan their lives by it , and that the boundaries of legal duty are set by a Parliament voters elect and can remove .
