@@137166266 <h> Is the climate law change this government 's jump the shark moment ? <p> Paul Goldsmith has kicked off a storm ( Photo : Getty . Collage : The Spinoff ) <p> The government has ignored international law , the world 's highest court , our own Supreme Court and the science . <p> There 's a concept in TV called jumping the shark . It 's the moment where a show loses all credibility because it gets so outlandish the audience can no longer suspend disbelief . When script writers , desperate to maintain popularity , do something so spectacularly stupid , they cement their downfall . <p> This week , the New Zealand government might well have jumped the shark when it announced plans to change the law to ensure no company can be held to account in court for the damage their emissions cause . To any New Zealander . Ever . <p> Apparently it was scared of what one man had set in motion . <p> Maori leader Mike Smith , elder of Ngapuhi and Ngati Kahu , took on seven of this country 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , acting as kaitiaki for his land and sea . When he took legal action in 2019 , no global court had yet endorsed the approach . Columbia University called his case " perhaps the biggest climate common law breakthrough " ever . In 2023 , the world 's leading climate scientists confirmed for the first time that climate litigation is an effective tool for cutting emissions . In 2024 , New Zealand 's own Supreme Court ruled unanimously that Smith 's case deserved its day in court . The trial was set for April 2027 . <p> Yet , this week , in the face of overwhelming global momentum , the New Zealand government decided to change the law , choosing to ignore international law , global scientific consensus , and cutting out our own Supreme Court . No consultation . No debate . Just a quiet announcement it might have hoped we 'd miss in a sea of bad environmental decisions . <p> Smith himself put it plainly : " If Parliament can cancel a live court case , then no legal claim is secure at all , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ right . And this is n't just about climate . <p> The first companies the government 's law change will protect -- seven of New Zealand 's biggest emitters -- are collectively responsible for a third of the country 's emissions , and make billions in combined revenue . Fonterra alone made over $1bn profit last year . Fonterra , Genesis and Z Energy are all members of something brazenly called " the Climate Leaders Coalition " . It lobbied the government . <p> It 's tempting to assume that in making this decision , the government has done something stupid . But I think it 's much worse than stupidity . It knows the immense harm inaction on climate change is causing . And it simply does n't care . <p> Over the past two years in power the Luxon-led coalition has cancelled dozens of climate policies and plans . It axed the clean car discount and EV sales collapsed from one in five new cars to one in 13 . It abandoned plans to price agricultural emissions , even though National campaigned on a pricing system . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ban , making New Zealand the first country in the world to walk away from a commitment to phase out fossil fuels . It gutted climate reporting requirements for big business . It rejected every single recommendation from their own Climate Change Commission on emissions targets . Every one . <p> The Auckland floods ( Photo : supplied ) <p> The government has attempted to present itself as pragmatic -- the grownups in the room -- while ignoring scientific reports , global court rulings and the increasing cost to New Zealand livelihoods . The 2023 Auckland floods and Cyclone Gabrielle alone cost the New Zealand economy$9-$14bn . In Treasury 's own words : " These severe weather events are only going to get more frequent and worse . We 're experiencing the best weather we 're going to see in our lifetimes , now . " <p> The government 's own independent Climate Change Commission confirmed last month : climate impacts are hitting New Zealand sooner and more severely than expected . The Emissions Trading Scheme is failing . All four government carbon auctions in 2025 failed to sell a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Green co-leader Chl ? e Swarbrick says this government is using its " dying breaths " to remove New Zealanders ' right to hold emitters accountable . The courts ca n't intervene , it just made sure of that . The only thing that can change this is a new government reversing it . <p> Mike Smith showed us what one person acting with conviction can do . He nearly had this country 's biggest polluters in a courtroom . So our government changed the law . But this global movement is bigger than one case and it is not stopping . <p> Christopher Luxon and his friends may be counting on enough confusion , enough cynicism , enough exhaustion for this to slide . They 've ignored international law , the world 's highest court , our own Supreme Court , their own commission , and the science . Whether that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> " Climate litigation has emerged as a tool for individuals , civil society and governments to challenge inadequate action on climate change by both public institutions and private entities . This tool first came into use in 1986 . Since that point it has grown considerably , especially post 2015 and the advent of the Paris Agreement . As of the middle of 2025 , the cumulative number of cases includes an estimated 3,099 climate change cases filed in 55 jurisdictions and 24 international or regional courts , tribunals , quasi-judicial bodies or other adjudicatory bodies . <p> " New Zealand is part of this tide of which there have been a series of important cases . While some of these have failed others have found openings , which plaintiffs seek to utilise to force countries/entities to do more to respond what is an undoubtedly a very serious time in climate change matters . Within New Zealand , some of these attempts are utilising aspects of Tort law , with the tools adopted ( such as nuisance and negligence ) representing hundreds of years of careful evolution through the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ should only be done with extreme caution . Some of the cases are also trying to advance novel areas of tort responsibilities in the area of climate change . This use of novel extensions to ensure entities are held accountable for their actions is a long tradition in tort law . <p> " The challenge for the government is that some current litigation may utilise the existing laws and apply them to climate change responsibilities of large emitting entities , making them responsible to do more to avert climate change . It is quite possible that some of the claims currently being advanced have merit and could impose costs on the named entities to do more to reduce their climate impacts . Rather than have this theory tested in court , the government seems intent on removing all possibilities of judgements in this area being reached . @@137186821 <p> The Government is facing growing criticism after changes to environmental law aimed at limiting climate-related lawsuits triggered concern from legal experts and environmental advocates . <p> The proposed legal changes are designed to restrict the ability of groups and individuals to take climate-related cases through the courts , particularly cases seeking accountability over emissions and environmental harm . <p> Critics say the move could weaken legal pathways for communities , iwi and environmental groups trying to hold major polluters and government agencies accountable for climate impacts . <p> The changes come as climate litigation continues to grow internationally , with courts increasingly being used to challenge governments and corporations over their response to climate change and environmental damage . <p> Environmental law experts say the Government 's approach risks undermining access to justice and reducing public oversight at a time when climate impacts are becoming more severe across Aotearoa . <p> The legal debate follows ongoing court action in New Zealand over the Government 's climate policies and emissions reduction plans . Earlier this year , climate advocacy groups challenged the Government 's decision to roll back several environmental @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cases have become an increasingly important tool globally , with lawsuits targeting governments and fossil fuel companies over issues including emissions , climate inaction and environmental harm . <p> Advocates say Maori communities are among those most vulnerable to climate change impacts , including coastal erosion , flooding , biodiversity loss and threats to whenua and wai . <p> Legal commentators warn limiting the courts ' role in climate disputes could reduce opportunities for affected communities to challenge decisions that impact future generations and the environment . <p> The Government says the changes are intended to provide greater legal certainty and reduce what it sees as costly and disruptive litigation against businesses and development projects . <p> Environmental groups , however , argue the reforms could prioritise commercial interests over climate responsibility and weaken protections at a time when stronger action on emissions and sustainability is being demanded both domestically and internationally . 
@@137199990 <h> Why this AI data centre might be New Zealand 's most important climate project <p> Running the Datagrid data centre in New Zealand rather than China could save the equivalent annual emissions of half a million cars . <p> For the last decade , tech evangelists have stared at lush green farmland on the outskirts of Invercargill and dreamed of covering it in grey warehouses full of servers . Because of the area 's cold climate , high rainfall and proximity to hydroelectric dams , Southland has long been posited as an ideal environment for a large-scale data centre industry . <p> The first major test of that thesis is under way now . In March , Environment Southland granted resource consent for the Datagrid Data Centre Park in Makarewa , about 7km from Invercargill . It will span 49 hectares of farmland and cost $3.5 billion to build . <p> It will be by far the largest data centre ever built in New Zealand -- and the first in the South Island that meets the technical requirements of hyperscale companies like Amazon , Meta , Google and Microsoft @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ New Zealand 's current total compute capacity by 65% when it opens in 2028 . It will be the second-largest energy user in the country after Tiwai Point Aluminium Smelter , consuming more each year than the entire city of Wellington . It 's permitted to draw up to 220 million litres of groundwater per year -- roughly equivalent to seven Southland dairy farms . It will also destroy a nearby wetland ( which Environment Southland says is of " very low ecological value " ) and is likely to disturb native marine species at Oreti Beach when it is connected to a new undersea cable . <p> Datagrid is majority-owned by Remi Galasso , a French-born tech entrepreneur , and is expected to create 1,200 short-term jobs during the construction phase , as well as requiring an ongoing staff of 50 people . The company has signed a 15-year agreement with Mercury to supply 140mw of power per year , about half of what the data centre requires . <p> Mercury Energy 's Kaiwera Downs Wind Farm will contribute power to the new data centre ( Source : ElectroNet @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ did n't prompt strong opposition from environmental groups , though a public petition on Change.com calling to stop the project topped 26,000 signatures . Some of the comments highlighted the risks of power or water shortages but many more clearly responded on an emotional level . <p> Around the world there is a growing resentment towards data centres , big tech and AI in general . A 2025 Ipsos survey found that 66% of New Zealanders are nervous about AI and that 84% prefer articles , images and videos that are made by humans rather than AI ( the highest rate of all surveyed countries ) . <p> There are many valid concerns with AI , and it 's important to debate how they can be addressed through regulation , especially when it comes to consumer access to water and power . But the data centres could also present an opportunity for climate change mitigation and New Zealand 's renewable energy industry . <p> Source : MBIE Energy Quarterly . <p> The International Energy Agency reported that " data centres connected to electricity grids with lower shares of generation based @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ enormous potential to help ( or hinder ) global clean energy transitions " . <p> Based on average emissions intensity of electricity production , powering a data centre the size of Datagrid in the US would produce about one million tonnes of Co2 annually , even though many data centres there claim to be zero-emission due to buying carbon credits . In China it would produce about 1.4 million tonnes . A fully coal-powered facility , like many of the Chinese-owned centres in Inner Mongolia , would emit as much as two million tonnes . <p> In Southland , running on hydropower and wind energy , its emissions will be virtually zero . Of course , this is a best-case scenario . It could cause some indirect emissions if it causes power shortages that need to be made up for elsewhere using fossil fuels . <p> Emissions intensity from energy production in various leading economies ( Source : Ember ) <p> The annual carbon emissions saving from running Datagrid in New Zealand rather than China could be equivalent to taking almost half a million cars off the road . Ten @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ industry . Forty would offset New Zealand 's entire carbon footprint . You do n't have to like AI to agree that it is better to run it on hydropower than coal . Even if we ca n't slow the advancement of technology , we can do our bit to lessen its impact on the environment . <p> The technological breakthrough that makes this possible is n't anything to do with LLMs or machine learning . It 's the cable : the Tasman Ring Network , a 6,000km subsea cable that will give South Island a direct connection to Australia rather than routing everything through Auckland . Datagrid claims it will reduce latency by up to 35% , which is essential for AI workloads . Without that connection , Southland could never credibly compete for contracts from major firms . <p> A render of the Tasman Ring Network ( Image : Datagrid ) <p> That means for the first time New Zealand will be able to " export " renewable energy produced in the South Island to international customers by letting them run their most energy-intensive processes on our zero-emissions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ oil reserve but no way of exporting it . Then someone built a pipeline connecting it to the world . And unlike most oil reserves , this one will never run out . There is almost unlimited capacity to build more dams , solar farms and wind turbines but it has been held back by a lack of demand in the domestic market . No major power plant has been built since the Clyde Dam in 1992 . No one wants to spend millions of dollars producing energy they wo n't be able to sell . <p> The Clyde hydro dam ( Photo : Supplied ) <p> The data centre industry can offer a predictable source of demand that has completely reshaped the fundamentals of the renewable energy market . Rather than producing just enough power to keep New Zealand running , the gentailers now have a profit incentive to generate more power and sell it to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Contact Energy 's Te Huka Unit 3 geothermal power station near Taupo was built because of backing from Microsoft for its Auckland data centre region . Mercury 's Turitea South wind farm was supported by a contract from Amazon Web Services . The current buildout is adding new generation capacity around 25% faster than the Think Big era of the 1970s and 1980s . <p> Source : Energy New Zealand <p> The construction of new data centres and power plants will undeniably cause some environmental harm . @@137200089 <p> Australia witnessed a political earthquake last weekend . A by-election for the federal seat of Farrer was won in a landslide by the populist , anti-immigration One Nation Party . <p> New Zealanders can be forgiven for having no interest in Australian by-elections . But the result in Farrer warrants their attention . <p> Farrer is predominantly a rural seat covering 127,000 sq km in southwestern New South Wales . Significantly , it 's been held by the centre-right Liberal/Nationals Coalition since its creation in 1949 . <p> Just twelve months ago , in the 2025 national election , Farrer was easily won by the Liberal Party . Last Saturday , the Liberal candidate slumped to third place , and One Nation took the seat by a wide margin . <p> This is the first time One Nation has won a seat in Australia 's lower house . <p> Who is One Nation and why is its win so significant ? <p> One Nation was founded in 1997 by Pauline Hanson , a Senator for Queensland since 2016 , and a controversial figure in Australian politics for decades . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a full burqa in the Senate on two occasions . <p> It 's a ' populist ' party , in the sense that it offers simple solutions to complex problems and cultivates an attitude of ' us vs them ' . It 's also a ' nationalist ' party that promotes allegiance to Australia and strongly opposes immigration . <p> Its immigration policy includes deporting 75,000 ' illegal immigrants ' , introducing an 8-year waiting period for citizenship and welfare , refusing entry to migrants from nations ' known to foster extremist ideologies ' , and withdrawing from the UN Refugee Convention . <p> Timing is everything in politics and One Nation 's victory in Farrer together with its rapid rise in the polls in recent months indicates the party 's immigration policies are now gaining traction with more and more voters . There are two primary reasons for this . <p> The first is economic . The One Nation narrative blames migrants for housing shortages , inner-city congestion , and stretched public services , issues that concern many Australians . <p> The second is cultural . The Israel/Gaza crisis @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and heightened tension between Muslim and Jewish communities . That tension has been exacerbated by the Bondi terrorist attack last December and the ongoing Royal Commission on Antisemitism and Social Cohesion . <p> Many voters oppose immigrants who bring their foreign conflicts with them to Australia and believe that the values of some immigrants are inconsistent with ' Australian values ' . <p> But it would be foolish to assume that the rise of One Nation is just about immigration . <p> There are broader forces at play here . A decline of trust in institutions , a growing sense of grievance and resentment , and a rejection of inner-city progressivism . Perhaps most tellingly a belief among many Australians that the political system no longer works for them and that they 've been forgotten by the politicians . <p> This phenomenon is hardly unique to Australia . The same trends are obvious in the United States with the emergence of the MAGA movement . They 're also present in many other western countries e.g. the rise of the Reform Party in the UK , the AfD in Germany , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of those countries , Australia is seeing a growing number of disaffected citizens turn away from the parties that have previously dominated politics . The old two-party system -- Labor vs the Liberal/Nationals Coalition -- is breaking down . <p> A key factor in that breakdown is the fact that political ideology no longer fits neatly along the traditional single left/right axis -- a right-wing business/middle class party competing against a left-wing workers ' party . <p> There are now ( at least ) two separate axes -- cultural and economic . If you include an environmental axis the position becomes even more complex . <p> This explains the ongoing fragmentation of Australian politics and the emergence of what is rapidly becoming a five- or six-party system -- Labor , the Greens , One Nation , the ' Teal ' group of MPs , the Liberals , and the Nationals . <p> There 's a party for every combination of ideological positions . <p> One Nation sits on the cultural right but is centre left on the economy . And it strongly opposes action on climate change . <p> The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ its supporters as racist . But the Farrer result and recent polling suggest that approach is becoming untenable . <p> Some of One Nation 's positions represent a clear threat to both the major parties . For example , its cultural conservativism has appeal to many blue-collar voters who are an important part of Labor 's base , and to many older voters who are an important part of the Liberal base . <p> Is the rise of One Nation relevant to New Zealand ? It certainly ca n't be dismissed given that a similar trend has emerged in many other liberal democracies . And the MMP system does facilitate minority party representation . <p> The nearest kiwi equivalent to One Nation is NZ First , a populist , nationalist party but without the hard anti-immigrant edge . <p> The closest NZ politics has come to the One Nation vibe was the vehement reaction in 2023 to Three Waters and the Labour policy of co-governance . A revival of that policy or other Treaty of Waitangi initiatives might trigger a surge in right-wing populism . <p> Immigration is not currently @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ if there was an extended period of economic decline . @@137213599 <h> Share this article <p> Reminder , this is a Premium article and requires a subscription to read . <p> Endurance flights of bar-tailed godwits are among the most finely tuned adaptations to seasonal changes . Photo / Getty Images <p> Endurance flights of bar-tailed godwits are among the most finely tuned adaptations to seasonal changes . Photo / Getty Images <p> Seasonal changes are Earth 's most fundamental rhythm . The annual pattern of seasons prescribes when plants flower and fruits ripen , animals mate and migrate , snow melts , monsoon rains fall and polar ice waxes or wanes -- even when kids play rugby or cricket . <p> " Seasonal fluctuations occur in almost every ecosystem , " says Daniel Hern ? ndez-Carrasco , an ecologist at the University of Canterbury . Hern ? ndez-Carrasco was announced last month as New Zealand 's national champion in the 2026 Frontiers Planet Prize , for his research exploring the consequences of increasingly unpredictable seasons and how impacts cascade through ecosystems . <p> Species have evolved many different adaptations to synchronise their activities with the natural rhythms of their environments @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ world warms , leaving many species out of sync with the resources they need to survive . " We rely on calendars to time our activities , but other species rely on evolutionary adaptations that can be much more difficult to update as seasonality changes . " <p> Although the disruption of Earth 's natural seasons is becoming ever clearer , Hern ? ndez-Carrasco says the flow-on effects on biodiversity and food systems are often overlooked consequences of climate change . And we are likely underestimating them . <p> Long-distance animal migrations , such as the trans-hemispheric endurance flights of bar-tailed godwits , are among the most finely tuned adaptations to seasonal changes . As animals move around the globe in pursuit of conditions that provide either plentiful feeding grounds or good breeding conditions , even a small seasonal shift can have a large impact . <p> Hibernation is another example of ecological patterns and relationships between different species that follow Earth 's ancient seasonal rhythm . In Australia , when the critically endangered mountain pygmy possum emerges from its winter slumber , one of its main food sources is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the Australian Alps seeking shelter for the summer . Successive droughts linked to climate change have decimated the moth . <p> Another crucial adaptation is the change in fur thickness most mammals undergo . Species that live in places with winter snow , such as Northern Hemisphere populations of stoats , also change their fur colour to be better camouflaged when they hunt or are hunted . This is an example where a seasonal mismatch is becoming obvious . Warmer spring temperatures trigger earlier snowmelt but the stoats rely on shifts in daylight hours as a cue to change colour . As a result , they are more exposed to predators . <p> Hern ? ndez-Carrasco also studies more localised human impacts on seasons , including the damming of rivers -- which often abruptly and completely disrupts natural flows -- and deforestation in the tropics , which changes the timing of the onset of the rainy season . <p> Climate change has a compounding effect , he says , by systematically modifying seasonal patterns in snow cover and rainfall around the world . <p> Seasonal disruptions add yet another threat @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ecological processes we rely on . A mismatch between plankton blooms and the life cycles of fish could affect fisheries . When flowering is out of sync with the emergence of pollinating insects , food production becomes less reliable . Even the regulation of the climate system itself is tightly controlled by seasonal processes . <p> Hern ? ndez-Carrasco is one of 25 national champions in the running for the Swiss-based Frontiers organisation 's annual award of US$1 million to each of three finalists to further research aimed at helping humanity to stay within our planet 's environmental limits . If he were to win , he says he would focus on overcoming the research challenges to studying seasonal shifts in natural ecosystems where many things happen all at once , making it difficult to isolate specific impacts or mechanisms . <p> Recognition as a national winner has already helped bring attention to shifting seasonality , allowing him to present his work outside the usual scientific circles , including at the World Economic Forum . <p> Changes to seasons matter because " the stability and performance of ecosystems -- and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rhythms . "

@@137248469 <p> Comment : The Government 's plan to limit climate civil litigation has created ambiguity and uncertainty about how major climate emitters will be held legally accountable for their contributions to climate harm . <p> Justice Minister Paul Goldsmith announced on May 12 that the Government intended to amend the Climate Change Response Act 2002 , with the aim of limiting and potentially foreclosing climate change litigation , like Smith v Fonterra . <p> The stated rationale is to reduce exposure to such claims to provide greater commercial certainty for greenhouse gas emitters . <h> read more <p> At one level , that is consistent with other policy settings . Businesses value predictability , and governments often seek to reduce legal risk to encourage investment . But this trade-off is questionable . <p> While much remains unclear about the scope of the proposed amendment , the Government appears willing to exchange commercial certainty for environmental insecurity . That is not just a technical legal adjustment ; it is a choice about how we confront one of the defining long-term challenges of our time . <p> The Smith v Fonterra case @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Forum Michael Smith ( Ngapuhi , Ngati Kahu ) against several major emitters . Smith is attempting to use tort law to address the diffuse , cumulative harms of climate change to his property , culture , and iwi . <p> Tort law typically deals with situations where harm is direct , identifiable , and attributable to a specific defendant . If a neighbour 's tree falls on your house , responsibility is relatively clear . <p> Climate change , however , does not fit neatly into that model . Even though emissions come from a point source , they are globally dispersed . Specific attribution , or ' causation ' become more challenging . Also , the harm is collective as well as individual . <p> Smith v Fonterra pushes tort law into unfamiliar territory by asking whether major emitters can be held legally accountable for their contribution to climate harm , even where no single act can be tied to a specific injury in a conventional sense . <p> Back us when it really matters <p> Donate or subscribe today to support local independent journalism . <p> For @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for only $199 ( saving $91 ) and get 12 months access ( for two users ) . <p> For that reason , the case has always sat uneasily within orthodox legal doctrine . Whether such claims would ultimately succeed remains uncertain . But their significance lies in the attempt to use the common law to grapple with a problem that existing regulatory frameworks have not fully addressed . <p> The Government 's proposed amendment appears designed to shut down this line of litigation . If enacted as suggested , it would remove the possibility of using civil litigation to pursue climate-related claims against emitters . Parliament is , of course , entitled to define the scope and limits of liability . <p> Our Accident Compensation Scheme is a well-known example . That legislation removed individual rights to sue in personal injury in exchange for a comprehensive no-fault system of support . <p> But that analogy only goes so far . In this case , the right to sue may be removed without any comparable substitute . This carries wider concerns . <p> First , the very possibility of litigation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ about responsibility , harm , and accountability can be tested . Closing off that avenue suggests that certain kinds of environmental grievances will not be recognised within the legal system . It removes one mechanism for protecting rights and property , even as the Government asserts that property and rights are one of its priorities . <p> Second , it is a signal internationally . Climate litigation is gaining traction worldwide . New Zealand 's developments have been closely watched . In announcing that it will foreclose present and future climate tort claims , the Government is signalling that New Zealand is no longer participating in this area of legal innovation . That may reassure some investors , but it also raises a tough question : Who wants to invest in an increasingly insecure environment ? <p> The timing of the announcement is also notable . Just days earlier , the Climate Change Commission released the second National Climate Change Risk Assessment as required by the Climate Change Response Act 2002 . That report outlines the scale of the risks New Zealand faces as a result of climate change , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , a move to limit legal accountability for emissions appears , at best , incongruous . <p> There are legitimate concerns about using tort law to address climate change . Courts are not always well equipped to resolve highly complex , diffuse issues involving science , economics , and policy . There is a risk of judicial overreach , and of imposing liability in ways that are difficult to calibrate fairly . <p> But the alternative is not simply to close the door . If the statutory framework is not fully fit for purpose , then the common law has a role to play in probing its limits . At present , New Zealand lacks a comprehensive climate change plan and does not allocate responsibility for the costs of climate harm . <p> If litigation is foreclosed , a question arises : who pays ? The costs of climate harms will fall on someone . Is the burden to fall on taxpayers , rather than on those whose activities contribute the most damage ? Is this just another example of privatising profit and socialising losses ? <p> Cases like Smith @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ institutions to respond . The Government is indicating that Smith v Fonterra is problematic and the existing legislation is sufficient . That is contestable . <p> The breadth of the proposed amendment is a significant issue . We currently do not know how far it will go in insulating emitters from liability or what forms of litigation it will capture . Reform aimed at creating certainty for business risks creating new uncertainties elsewhere , about rights , remedies , responsibility , and the future direction of climate governance . <p> Ultimately , the Government 's position can be distilled to a simple proposition : reducing legal risk for emitters is a priority . <p> But this sits uneasily alongside the realities of climate change . Economic activity does not exist independently of environmental conditions and those conditions are becoming more volatile . We have recently seen indications of this often , across flood-prone and coastal regions , and most recently in Northland . <p> In that sense , the proposed reform does more than tidy up an awkward corner of tort law . It prioritises short-term business interests over the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Whether that is a worthwhile trade is a question deserving of intense public scrutiny . <p> Associate professor Ben France-Hudson researches the law and theory of private property , with a particular focus on natural resources , land law and climate change , at the University of Otago 's Faculty ... The battle of the thermostat , complete with passive-aggressive emails , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and down throughout the day . @@137289212 <h> Share this article <p> Reminder , this is a Premium article and requires a subscription to read . <p> People are heading for the door in record numbers . What can be done to stop it swinging back and hitting NZ in the face ? Photo / Getty Images <p> People are heading for the door in record numbers . What can be done to stop it swinging back and hitting NZ in the face ? Photo / Getty Images <p> No one knows what 's causing the global decline in birth rates -- " the death of birth " as some of the scare stories like to call it . It 's not climate change , secularism , capitalism , birth control , wokeness or the Atlas Network . <p> The shift from large to small families began in Francein the early-19th century ; it spread across Europe , the West and now it 's nearly everywhere : Israel is the only wealthy country above replacement level . In 1926 , New Zealand 's total fertility rate ( the average number of births per woman ) was 2.88 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ year it was 1.55 , a point at which each subsequent generation is 25% smaller than the previous one . <p> We do n't fixate on fertility the way we dwell on unemployment , inflation , or growth , but long term , it 's probably the most consequential statistic in our politics . <p> For decades New Zealand , like most wealthier nations , has relied on immigration to balance out our demographic decline and keep our economy afloat , and this is hitting the wall with the rise of the populist right . <p> New Zealand First refused to support our free trade deal with India on the dubious grounds that it would lead to higher immigration -- Shane Jones is boldly taking a stand against allowing more trained cardiologists , aged-care workers and software engineers into the country . Act just released its immigration policy , which links immigrant numbers to infrastructure capacity and " Kiwi character " . In his pre-budget speech , Christopher Luxon signalled a tightening of immigration policy . <p> Last week , the Reform UK Party made huge gains in Britain 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ surprise victory in the Farrer by-election . Centrist parties are either pivoting on immigration -- like the Albanese government -- or being trampled in the dust by the populists ascendent , like the regime of UK Prime Minister Keir Starmer . <p> Source : RBNZ statistical series M12 / Seasonally adjusted <h> Too few immigrants <p> How do we fund the state if we reduce immigration ? Treasury 's recent Long-Term Fiscal Statement projected government debt to hit 200% of GDP by 2065 , primarily driven by the rising costs of superannuation and healthcare . But even this grim forecast assumes 1 million additional new immigrants arriving over the next 25 years . Without them it 's not clear that the current system is affordable for even half that period . <p> In the past few weeks , Luxon and Chris Hipkins have been murmuring about superannuation reform . In the muted tones of a pair of decrepit monks beseeching a horde of Vikings to stop burning their monastery and slaughtering the nuns , they have gently suggested that New Zealanders vote to roll back their current entitlements . Hipkins @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ National wants to raise the age of eligibility . <p> Both of them disagree with each other 's solutions , and both insist there must be consensus on such a vital issue . Winston Peters ( let us imagine him as a Viking shaman -- a cunning man ; a robe of stitched animal pelts over his suit , a dear-antler helmet covering his perfectly arranged hair , urging his warriors to pillage and destroy to their hearts ' content ) has sternly ruled out age eligibility and means testing , and pledged to protect superannuation in its current form . " This will not change . " <p> Something will have to give here , and Peters offered us a preview : this year 's Budget will scrap Labour 's fees-free scheme , which funded final-year fees for tertiary students . <p> On one hand this was a terrible policy -- a massive wealth transfer to high-income households , a bleak reminder of what a wretchedly terrible education minister Chris Hipkins was . On the other , this was one of the few tangible benefits that went to young @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ our tax and transfer system means the state 's revenue is over-reliant on a narrow band of high-income earners with few or no children . If you 're a young member of this cohort contemplating the low wages , long hours , unaffordable housing market and the gloomy economic prospects New Zealand offers , this looks less like a country with a social contract and reciprocal welfare state and more like an intergenerational pyramid scheme . <p> These workers are leaving at rates last seen after the Global Financial Crisis , and the logic behind Peters ' intractability on superannuation combined with the increasing cost of health will drive governments towards the gradual cannibalisation of the rest of the state as the only way to fund the scheme in its current form . Which will drive out more of the people who pay for it all . <h> Grand coalition ? <p> The impossibility of all this is behind some of the recent discourse about a grand coalition : a cinematic crossover event in which National and Labour band together combining their powers to solve all our problems , unfettered @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> But there have been cross-party agreements negotiated on housing and climate in recent years and Luxon defected from both of them . Labour would be very foolish to trust any arrangement with National 's current leader . <p> And it 's less than three years since Hipkins was the prime minister of an absolute majority government and he conspicuously did not solve all our problems . The Hipkins-Luxon show would look more like Waiting for Godot than a superhero movie ( " Let 's fix our productivity decline . " " Yes . " They do not move . ) <p> Both leaders have been told to promise voters they 'll solve our problems because that 's the thing you say , but neither offers up any meaningful solutions . In the absence of a credible alternative , older voters are backing New Zealand First because it will -- at least -- protect their interests in the immediate short-term . @@137289227 <p> Book people are the best . Every year the tens of thousands of people from the very young to the very old who fill Aotea Center for the Auckland Writers Festival ( AWF ) remind me that people not only care about books but they care enough to spend money ( or attend one of the many free events ) to listen to discussions about them . AWF 's talented , always-smiling and serene artistic director Lyndsey Fineran told the crowd at the festival 's gala night that 2026 has seen the best sales figures in the festival 's history . The book sales across the festival 's three-day , 6,000+ student attendee schools programme were the highest ever , too . <p> In a time of political upheaval , declining rates of literacy and AI anxiety , this upward trajectory is extremely encouraging . Being in a venue filled with thousands of stimulated , excited , merry book lovers is a potent pick-me-up . I think it 's time other cities took note of AWF 's astonishing success built over years and years of hard work , reputation-building @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- they 're pop-up places where children and adults alike can see the magic of stories unfold in front of their eyes , receive lifelong advice , exercise their own minds and curiosities , participate in robust conversations that expand , educate and challenge . Book festivals build communities , sustain art and celebrate a breadth of stories and perspectives . They 're becoming , like libraries , an essential meeting place -- and if they 're well supported they will only grow in strength as local and global shifts and events inspire an urge to collectivise and gather and talk . <p> Hats off to the Auckland Writers Festival team and supporters who pulled off a phenomenal festival -- a highlight not only of Aotearoa 's literary calendar , but of the world's. / books editor Claire Mabey <p> Here is a round up of just a few of the events Spinoff staff members attended ( we have a male-heavy line-up of reviews here , which is purely accidental ) : <h> Patrick Radden Keefe <p> Jack Tame apologised before doing it , but then ploughed ahead and called @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a motherfucker . A skilled live storyteller ( so often those that can spin a yarn on paper can not do the same in person ) , he was quick-witted and ready to delve into the darkness and difficulty of his work . It 's a novel that splices family drama and domestic life with a newly unearthed Trojan epic and the academic analysis it warrants . Greek myths " marked " him from a young age , Martel told the audience at Kiri Te Kanawa Theatre on Friday evening . The Iliad is , he said , about dysfunctional men who ca n't get over their anger , describing the war as nihilistic and " curiously modern " . <p> In discussion with Paula Morris , he spoke of facts and their limitations , the " phenomenon of faith " and how humans are " post-rational " beings . " <p> Martel said he sees stories as a way to " understand life " . He writes to answer his own questions . " Every book of mine has been an attempt to understand something " . Each one is , he said , a quest . " I try to live a better life and be less fearful . " <p> What he said about books as a participatory medium really resonated ( a McLuhanism for those who subscribe to that ) . Martel said a book happens when you bring your life to it ; there 's a collaborative nature to reading fiction . Cerebral , empathetic and warm , I could have listened to him for hours but we only had one . <p> He discussed Son of Nobody , Life of Pi and High Mountains of Portugal . @@137289312 <h> Nicolas Jabko thinks US central bankers can no longer count on a deep well of conservative support for their independence <p> Public Policy / opinion <p> Nicolas Jabko thinks US central bankers can no longer count on a deep well of conservative support for their independence <p> 17th May 26 , 10:06ambyNicolas Jabko <p> Since Donald Trump 's return to the White House , he has relentlessly attacked the US Federal Reserve 's independence . In his pursuit -- so far fruitless -- of considerably lower interest rates , Trump has sought to intimidate and fire Fed officials and pack the central bank with loyalists . <p> Most remarkably , Trump has turned the Fed 's leadership succession into a political football . As part of the administration 's bid to jettison outgoing Fed Chair Jerome Powell , the US Department of Justice opened a baseless criminal investigation into Powell , which it closed only to ensure the confirmation of his successor , Kevin Warsh . <p> Such outright hostility toward the Fed may seem like a personal vendetta or an American oddity . But it is neither . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ support for independent central banks worldwide . <p> Central bankers became conservative heroes in the 1980s , when Paul Volcker was Fed Chair . Volcker was a Democrat , but he tamed runaway inflation with a series of aggressive rate hikes . Together with his calls to cut the federal deficit , Volcker 's conquest of monetary stability endeared him to conservatives . Building on Volcker 's legacy , his successor , Alan Greenspan , preached market liberalization to promote economic growth and contain inflationary pressures . <p> The establishment of the European Central Bank in 1998 marked the triumph of independent and inflation-focused central banks . With the support of many politicians and economists ( including liberals as well as staunch conservatives ) , these institutions gained more prominence . Backed by financial markets , informal alliances supporting low inflation and neoliberal policies then became entrenched in international policy circles . At the turn of the 21st century , critics of independent central banks and their priorities were found almost exclusively on the left . <p> All that changed after the global financial crisis of 2008 -- well before @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the Fed under Ben Bernanke rescued Wall Street , cut interest rates to zero , and launched massive purchases of financial assets , known as quantitative easing . Other central banks around the world adopted similar unconventional policies , although their timing and scale differed . <p> This gave rise to a new breed of critics on the right . In the United States , Tea Party activists and politicians accused Bernanke of leading America to ruin . They complained that the Fed should fight inflation and fiscal deficits , not bail out banks or facilitate credit easing . Some conservative monetary policymakers , economists , and financiers also turned against the Fed 's huge stimulus . These establishment critics notably included Warsh , the incoming Fed chair , and Scott Bessent , now Trump 's treasury secretary . In their view , the Fed was encroaching on fiscal policy and distorting financial markets . <p> These criticisms fueled the radicalized conservative movement that elevated Trump to the presidency in 2016 . MAGA Republicans share a hatred of the " deep state " and a distrust of independent government @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ expect his Fed nominees to slash interest rates when he was in power , and to raise them when he was not . Trump soured on Powell , his pick for Fed chair in 2017 , for not following that script . <p> European conservatives have also become more critical of central banks , as they have competed with increasingly popular far-right parties in recent years . Germany 's far-right party Alternative f ? r Deutschland was founded by conservatives who opposed the euro and demanded austerity in Greece and other European Union countries . The United Kingdom Independence Party and its successor , Reform UK , successfully advocated an exit from the EU and railed against the Bank of England . <p> To be sure , conservative supporters of independent central banks have not disappeared . In the US , some conservative Supreme Court justices and congressional Republicans resisted Trump 's assaults on Powell and the independent Fed . Likewise , many moderate conservatives in Europe have defended central bankers against attacks from the far right . But amid increased polarization , the process of appointing monetary policymakers is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ his part , has vowed to defend the Fed 's independence . But the question is whether he can do that without antagonizing Trump , who sees low interest rates as critical to boosting the economy and addressing affordability concerns before November 's midterm elections -- never mind the inflationary pressures caused by his tariffs and war against Iran . If Warsh 's Fed does not lower rates , and fast , Trump 's attacks are unlikely to cease . <p> The broader problem is that central bankers can no longer count on a deep well of conservative support for their independence and technocratic policymaking . In the US , Trump and his MAGA movement have made the Fed into a scapegoat , portraying it as biased against the administration 's agenda . In Europe , many conservatives have rebuffed central banks ' cautious efforts to address broad sources of instability , such as climate change , arguing that they should stick to their narrow mandate of ensuring price stability . <p> Even if the Fed and other central banks manage to preserve their institutional integrity , central bankers will @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ radicalized right without acquiescing to its partisan demands . @@137347234 <h> Crouch , pause , touch ... but no need to engage just yet <p> There is no need to rush decisions on local government change , Alan Somerville writes . <p> The government 's irresponsible announcement about local government changes on May 5 has sent councillors around the country into a spin . <p> It is irresponsible because it is pushing councils into making rushed decisions without carefully considering the issues and without properly consulting the people who will be affected . <p> Before anyone decides what the best local government structures might be for a region , let 's figure out a proper process for getting to that point . This is an important decision that will affect our communities for decades to come , and it 's a decision not to be made in a rush . <p> So what 's this all about ? <p> The government announced in November last year it wanted to see changes in local government . The emphasis was on council amalgamations and creating unitary authorities -- councils that combine both city or district council with regional council functions , such @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ preference seems to be a single unitary council covering a whole region . <p> Then in early May its updated plan appeared . Councils can submit proposals for change by August 9 in a ' ' Head Start ' ' process , and Cabinet will decide whether these are approved or not . Anyone else will enter a compulsory backstop process for change after the October 2028 local government elections . <p> Coming up with proposals now is getting ahead of ourselves . The first step is to agree on who should be involved in the decision-making and a process for carrying this through . <p> The decision-making group must be much wider than mayors and councillors alone . Mana whenua must be involved from the start because the change is crucially about environmental management . Our communities must also have a say because it is about how they are represented . <p> There are lots of complex issues here , particularly in a region as large and diverse as Otago . Current regional council functions around flood protection , natural hazards , freshwater quality and biodiversity have to be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ n't take notice of artificial boundaries . <p> How do we balance this incentive for region-wide scale with the need for local representation ? Can a single mayor and council properly represent communities as diverse as Queenstown , Ranfurly and Balclutha , or Dunedin and Kurow ? <p> The current regional boundaries may not be the best ones . There could be a case for combining Otago and Southland or even South Island functions , and for rationalising representation of the Waitaki catchment . We wo n't know unless we discuss and investigate this . <p> The government push is firmly for council amalgamations , so how will we figure things out fairly when councils have different levels of assets and debt ? The future ownership of Port Otago has already entered the conversation . There will also be value in retaining long-standing initiatives such as the ORC 's ECO Fund , which has been supporting community environmental projects since 2018 . <p> To further complicate matters councils are negotiating a great deal of change around water services , resource management reforms and the increasing effects of climate change . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ chief executives . <p> There is more uncertainty because the government made no effort to find cross-party support for its proposals around local government , so a change at November 's general election could mean another change of direction . <p> There is further danger in the government 's Head Start process . A group of councils can put forward a proposal that affects other councils or communities without their consent . <p> This is a recipe for ongoing discontent when it is an enduring solution we need . <p> Unfortunately , this latest round of change makes no attempt to deal with the underlying issue around local government funding . Local government 's share of GDP in New Zealand has stuck around 2% since before 1900 ( apart from a brief foray into the 4% zone during the Great Depression of the 1930s ) . <p> Over decades councils have been loaded with more responsibilities and expectations , while funding still relies predominantly on rates . <p> In Otago we can not reasonably meet the government 's three-month timeframe to get into the Head Start pathway . <p> We @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ states : ' ' Head Start is voluntary . Councils that do not opt in will not face mandatory change until after the 2028 local elections , giving them space to focus on delivering the first generation of plans under the new planning system . ' ' <p> Let us work towards having a good proposal finalised well before the 2028 local government elections , but not in three months . 
@@137347278 <h> Share this article <p> The New Zealand Government is planning to change the law to block civil claims seeking damages for harm caused by greenhouse gas emissions . Photo / Mark Mitchell <p> The New Zealand Government is planning to change the law to block civil claims seeking damages for harm caused by greenhouse gas emissions . Photo / Mark Mitchell <p> The Government 's plan to change the law to bar claims for harms from greenhouse gas emissions shuts down New Zealand 's most important climate tort case , meaning it will never be decided on its merits . <p> The move overrides a unanimous Supreme Court decisionthat Smith v Fonterra , a case against some of New Zealand 's major corporate emitters , should go to trial . <p> It also guts the future capacity of tort law -- a branch of civil law allowing people to seek damages for harm caused by wrongful actions by others -- to curb climate harms . <p> Justice Minister Paul Goldsmith justified it by saying climate change is best managed by the Government at a national level , not through @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " provide businesses with certainty around their obligations " . <h> What the courts do that politics ca n't <p> In New Zealand and elsewhere , tort law is being used to hold greenhouse gas emitters to account . <p> Tort claimants are also targeting governments for not doing enough to protect current and future generations . Leaving climate change regulation entirely to the Government , then , is a little like putting the fox in charge of the hen house . <p> The minister is right to say the fight against climate change needs robust government action . But removing the role of the courts is the wrong way to go about it . <p> Courts do things governments can not . They can not be lobbied . They decide on evidence and law . They name specific actors and test their conduct against legal standards . <p> No one denies the complexities of fighting climate change . But tort law has always confronted new challenges . <p> It has been part of the fight against factory pollution , defective products , unsafe workplaces and corporate fraud . In @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ against online harm . Climate change is next in that sequence . <p> Courts overseas are already doing this work . In 2021 , a Dutch court ordered a multinational company to align its activities with the Paris Agreement , the main international treaty combating climate change . However , this decision was appealed in 2024 . <p> Last year , a German court held that major emitters can be liable for climate harm , even when the harm occurs abroad . <p> In Australia , torts claimants have attempted to hold the Government to account for inadequate climate regulation . Some claims have been rejected , but one is ongoing . <p> The courts confirmed the climate science and increased public awareness . With the Smith v Fonterra case , we were about to see how New Zealand tort law can contribute to these efforts . <h> Why the Government 's argument is wrong <p> We also call the Government 's " certainty for business " argument into question . <p> Uncertainty is not effectively managed by extinguishing risks . Investors need risks to be priced . Legislating away @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it to others , just as lax regulation of flood risk has imposed severe costs on councils , insurers and households . <p> Under the Government 's law change proposal , those who profit most from lax climate policy would be insulated from paying their share . <p> Against that backdrop , preventing climate-focused tort claims is not an outlier . We argue it is the next layer of a deliberate dismantling of a system of climate accountability . <p> This conflicts with constitutional and international legal recognition that governments have a duty to protect citizens from climate change . <p> Last year , the International Court of Justice issued an advisory opinion which unanimously affirmed that states have legal obligations to protect the climate system , and that failing to prevent significant harm creates responsibility under international law . <p> The German Federal Constitutional Court also said Germany 's constitution requires the government to safeguard future generations from worsening impacts of climate change . <p> New Zealand 's current political process is not holding major emitters to account , and the regulatory framework is straining . The courts were @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- until now . <p> This article is republished from The Conversation under a Creative Commons licence . Read the original article at the above link . 
@@137347493 <p> The primary sector is leading New Zealand 's economic recovery , according to economist and researcher Cameron Bagrie . <p> But what he calls ' jandal economics ' is not helping the recovery . <p> He told Rural News that overall the primary sector is doing pretty well now due to a combination of good commodity prices and the weakness of the NZ dollar . <p> He says while the sector is having to face up to the same challenges as the rest of society , it is doing well . <p> These challenges include what ability the primary sector has to pass increased costs on to consumers and what resistance there might be to this . <h> Agricultural Productivity Outperforming Wider Economy <p> " But the strength of the primary sector lies in its strong productivity story . Productivity in agriculture in NZ is typically 1.5% to 2%R for the past decades has been 0.3% . That tells you that there are an awful lot of sectors across NZ that have negative productivity and that 's a major economic problem , " he says . <p> Bagrie believes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ income crisis rather than what is often termed a cost of living crisis . <p> He says when there is low productivity , there are low wages , and when this happens it is much harder to deal with because there is no flexibility . <h> NZ Faces an Income Crisis , Not Just a Cost of Living Crisis <p> He believes the biggest economic concern for NZ now is inflation , which hurts people , takes money out of their pockets and makes it harder for them to put food on the table . <p> " Inflation is not just issue number one by a small margin , it is issue number one by a big margin , " he says . <p> Bagrie says NZ politicians are facing the conundrum of having to deal which this short-term imperative instead of focusing on long-term strategic issues . He says there is a real tension between the two , but adds that politics tends to focus on the short-term . <p> " We 'd like to get people to take a deep breath and sit back and think about the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be addressing over the next ten to twenty years such as sustainability , climate change , productivity , and infrastructure ? But the stark reality is the cost of living is dominating the thinking , " he says . <h> Law of the Jandal <p> What NZ needs right now is certainty , says Bagrie , but he adds that is unlikely to happen soon due to wars and global political upheaval . <p> He says this is having an impact on politics here - especially as a general election looms towards the end of the year . <p> Bagrie notes that in the current polls the two main political parties , National and Labour , collectively have about 65% support with the remaining 35% spread amongst the minor parties . <p> With such a strong showing on the part of the peripheral parties , it leads to a situation where the tail is wagging the dog . <p> " We have got what I call ' jandal economics ' . There are more economic flip flops here than the flip flops that walk the beach at Bondi in Sydney @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ always lead to the best policy option being adopted , " he says . <p> The corollary of this says Bagrie is that it could lead to a series of one-term governments . <p> He points out that in the past , once elected , a party might reasonably expect to get at least two terms , but says now this may not be the case , a fact born out in recent elections overseas such as Australia and the UK where there is a rise in support for minor parties . <p> Like others , he notes the Irish situation where the two major centist parties , Fienna Fail and Fine Gael , formed an unlikely coalition to keep the periphery parties such as Sinn Fein and the Labour Party from having a say in the government . <p> " For this to happen here , both sides would have to swallow a few dead rats , " says Bagrie . <p> He says people are hurting in NZ and politics is being dominated by the polls and popularism . @@137347509 <p> A multibillion-dollar de-extinction company believes it is one step closer to resurrecting New Zealand 's largest and long-extinct flightless bird . <p> Colossal Biosciences - headquartered in Texas - is heralding a " major breakthrough " in its development of an artificial egg that it says is crucial to giant moa - which died out around 500 years ago - once again walking the earth . <p> The company said it had successfully hatched 26 chickens from a novel artificial egg , designed to be reproduced at scale and at any size . <p> Colossal 's Australia-based chief biology officer , Andrew Pask , is convinced a lack of understanding is the main thing standing in the way of public support and said while the company 's core goal is to restore species , it also wants to use its technology to prevent further extinctions . <p> Speaking to RNZ , Pask - who is also a professor at the University of Melbourne - said the company 's latest technological development was a significant step in solving the chicken and egg conundrum inherent in the moa project . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ( based on bone DNA ) and hopes to find it only differs by a few percentage points from the genome of its closest living relatives , emu and the South American tinamou . <p> After that , he said a living relative 's cells - the best candidate - could be genetically edited with moa-specific genes . <p> " Once we have that , the cell needs to be turned back into a living bird and for that , there 's currently no surrogate bird species that lays an egg big enough , so we had to make the Colossal artificial egg . " <p> He said the egg 's silicone-based membrane - a 3D-printed lattice shell - allowed oxygen to diffuse inside , overcoming a major design hurdle since first attempts in the 1980s . <p> Pask said previous artificial eggs required high levels of oxygen to be piped in , causing damage to the DNA and long-term health of the growing chick . <p> " So we had to engineer the artificial egg , really from the ground up again . <p> " We wanted to make @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ egg , because we want to be able to hatch birds that are going to have a normal lifespan , normal reproductive capacity , so that we can use this for conservation purposes as well as recreating the moa - so we need to be able to recreate that really large egg . " <p> The egg could be used in a standard incubator , and in addition to good gas-exchange across the egg 's ' shell ' the transparent design also enabled scientists - for the first time - to monitor the chick 's development in real-time , Pask said . <p> " All of those developments have been major , major breakthroughs . " <p> Pask said the project is on hold while they study the 26 chickens that have been hatched from the novel egg and provided they 're healthy with normal lifespans , the egg would then be " scaled up " to emu size . <p> The moa , however , was at least eight times larger in volume than an emu egg , he said . <p> " It 's an enormous egg , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ initial moa ... conceived inside an emu egg and then we 'd take that emu egg and put it the embryo into a large artificial egg which would be moa sized ... add extra yolk , extra albumen egg white ... to increase the volume of those components , so that it can then sustain the growth of a much larger chick , which is going to be our baby moa . " <p> Pask said it was hard to say when that would happen , but " less than 10 years , I 'd say - if you want a ballpark " . <p> In addition to the moa project , he hoped the artificial egg would act as a surrogate for critically endangered species - big or small . <p> " So bird species that are really struggling with reproduction or really struggling to breed easily in captivity - this could be an absolute game-changer for us , being able to create a lot of a particular species of bird that we 'd be able to release back into the wild and really help to boost population numbers @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ them a platform for early " genetic manipulations " on birds , possibly engineering them to be more resilient to disease or climate change . <p> In New Zealand , he said that could mean introducing genetic variation to species that had tiny populations . <p> " We can actually go back and sequence the DNA from animals that lived 50 , 100 , 150 years ago , have a look at the population diversity that they used to have and engineer that back in again , so you have really healthy breeding populations . @@137400638 <p> Environmental organisations , legal experts and academics are urging the Government to reverse proposed law changes they say would shield major greenhouse gas emitters from being held legally responsible for climate-related ... <p> Environmental organisations , legal experts and academics are urging the Government to reverse proposed law changes they say would shield major greenhouse gas emitters from being held legally responsible for climate-related harm . <p> The coalition of NGOs , climate scientists and legal academics says proposed amendments to the Climate Change Response Act would severely limit the ability of individuals , communities and iwi to take legal action against major polluters over damage linked to climate change . <p> The Government argues climate change liability issues should be managed through national climate policy and emissions regulation rather than through the courts . Ministers say tort law is not the appropriate mechanism for resolving complex climate-related disputes involving multiple industries and global emissions sources . <p> Much of the concern centres around the high-profile Smith v Fonterra case , brought by Maori climate advocate Mike Smith of Ngapuhi and Ngati Kahu , who is seeking to hold @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and its impacts on whenua , moana and future generations . The Supreme Court previously allowed the case to proceed , marking a potentially significant moment in international climate litigation . <p> Legal groups argue the Government 's proposed amendments would effectively shut down current and future climate-related lawsuits before courts have the opportunity to fully examine them . <p> Academics and environmental advocates also warn the move raises broader constitutional concerns about Parliament intervening to limit the courts ' role in developing common law responses to emerging crises such as climate change . <p> The Government maintains climate action is best delivered through existing emissions reduction plans and the Emissions Trading Scheme , rather than through costly and uncertain litigation . <p> Debate over the proposed law changes is expected to intensify as climate litigation becomes an increasingly important tool internationally for communities seeking accountability from governments and large corporations over climate harm .

@@137446397 <h> Pacific concerns about militarisation - and NZ 's role in it <p> New Zealand has been steadily pushing Pacific regional defence co-operation on a number of fronts . Photo : NZDF <p> New Zealand 's government is increasingly eager to promote the buy-in of Pacific nations for closer defence force integration in the region , amid concerns about militarisation of the region . <p> The security environment has been shifting rapidly , and regional defence is becoming more complex , leaving Pacific Islanders wondering if their " Ocean of Peace " is slipping out of their grasp . <p> In recent months , the defence and police forces of Australia and New Zealand have been increasing cooperation with counterparts in Pacific countries including Fiji , Tonga , Samoa and Vanuatu in efforts to combat transnational crime , especially the illicit drug trade . <p> But as a number of Pacific Island countries weigh up signing major bilateral treaties or agreements with the likes of Australia , China and the United States , New Zealand has been steadily pushing Pacific regional defence cooperation on a number of fronts . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Ministers Meeting ( SPDMM ) in Chile is instructive . <p> The SPDMM - which involves New Zealand , Australia , Chile , Fiji , France , Papua New Guinea and Tonga - notes the leadership role New Zealand has taken on better coordinating regional defence architecture . <p> New Zealand is contributing to the militarisation of the Pacific , says Pacific historian Marco de Jong . Photo : Supplied <p> The member countries agreed to push for a defence advisor from their collective to be embedded in the secretariat of the Pacific Islands Forum ( PIF ) , whose secretary-general Baron Waqa attended the Chile meeting and appeared to support closer integration . <p> While the advisor position is yet to be established , the SPDMM is surging ahead with a range of new regional defence initiatives , including developing the Pacific Response Group , under which defence personnel from Australia , Fiji , France , New Zealand and PNG work together to support coordinated humanitarian assistance and disaster relief responses across the Pacific . <h> Social licence <p> A briefing from March 's joint meeting of the Defence @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ how they aim to promote " the sense of integration through Pacific defence forces ' and to ' enhance the sense of Pacific forces meeting Pacific security needs " . <p> It also highlighted a keenness to " get more links between SPDMM and PIF so that these voices are heard directly by the region " . <p> " The briefings reveal New Zealand 's role in integrating and aligning Pacific defence forces alongside a considerable anxiety about regional social licence , " Pacific historian Marco de Jong said . <p> He said the language being used " speaks to a programme of influence and public relations , calibrated to downplay criticism that New Zealand is contributing to the militarisation of the Pacific " . <p> A representative with Development Alternatives with Women for a New Era , Maureen Penjueli , who is also a long time advocate for indigenous rights in the region , said there had been a lack of consultation with the wider Pacific Islands region about the new defence tack . <p> " We 've seen so much occupation by those in the defence interests area @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a very primary seat at the Pacific Islands Forum on security . We 've got competing interests , which is the Fusion Centre that 's in Vanuatu , " Penjueli said . <p> " When you add more and more players to this regional architecture that already has enough players on defence and security , it complicates the governance structure in a way . Who does it respond to ? " <p> Maureen Penjueli at a United States Institute of Peace 's panel discussion in October 2023 . @@137500454 <p> The very foundations of New Zealand 's foreign policy seem to be crumbling at a time when we need them the most . <p> For decades New Zealand has championed multilateralism as the backbone of its foreign policy , but in an increasingly fractured world , a gap is emerging between what we say and what we do . <p> Small states like New Zealand rely on multilateralism to amplify their voice and constrain great powers . But as global institutions weaken and strategic pressures rise , New Zealand is drifting toward a more selective and cautious form of engagement . <p> This is not a reflection of wavering commitment . It points to a deeper problem : the erosion of the system it relies on . <p> Small states like New Zealand can not rely on multilateralism alone in an increasingly unstable world . Recent actions by great powers have shown multilateral institutions are often ignored or bypassed . <p> In theory the United Nations provides a platform for states to collaborate on issues that do not respect borders , such as climate change and international terrorism . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ solve alone . <p> However , it is becoming increasingly obvious that great powers are indifferent to this reality . The United States and Russia have repeatedly denied the threat of climate change . <p> Both are permanent members of the United Nations Security Council ( UNSC ) who can veto collective action . Both have repeatedly used this power to block or dilute UN action on climate change and other international issues . <p> While the UN offers small states like New Zealand the chance to face our biggest problems collectively , these structural constraints raise questions about how effective that system remains in practice . <p> The 2003 US-led invasion of Iraq was widely considered to be an unprecedented moment for the UN , as it was an action initiated by a P-5 member without explicit UNSC authorisation . <p> Twenty-three years later the limitations of the system are once again under the spotlight . The US-Israel attacks on Iran leave small states like New Zealand wondering if the ' paper wall ' ' of the UN Charter is capable of constraining the unilateral actions of great powers @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rules begin to lose meaning , and small states have the most to lose . <p> As the world changes , New Zealand 's commitment to multilateralism has become more selective . While our leaders still talk like multilateralists , we act more cautiously when real costs are involved . <p> A big part of New Zealand 's foreign policy since the 1980s has been semi-detachment with great power , and a strong , independent voice on international issues . <p> New Zealand 's refusal to recognise Palestinian statehood in September last year completely undermines this approach to international relations . <p> This is seen to be an alignment with the United States and Israel on an issue about which 157 countries stand against us . <p> In this case , New Zealand 's actions do not match its stated principles . The gap between principle and practice raises questions about our level of commitment to multilateralism . <p> In falling back on a selective approach to an international rules-based order , New Zealand 's foreign policy risks becoming morally meaningless . Neutrality begins to look less like a principle @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the United Nations General Assembly , Winston Peters emphasised New Zealand 's desire to remain a friend to both Israelis and Palestinians . While this reflects a commitment to balance , it is inconsistent with New Zealand 's stated support for international law . <p> Where does this leave us then ? <p> If the rules only apply when great powers choose to follow them , then the system begins to look less like a safeguard and more like subservience . <p> For small states , that distinction matters . Multilateralism has never just been about co-operation , it has been about protection . <p> It is what allows countries like New Zealand to operate in a world dominated by far more powerful actors . <p> But if those actors can ignore the rules without consequence , then small states are left with a difficult choice . To continue to defend a system that is increasingly unable to defend them , or quietly adapt to a world where power , not principle , determines outcomes . <p> New Zealand can not afford to sit comfortably between those two positions , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ when it is tested , we should not be surprised that it fails us when it matters most . <p> * Logan Bryant is a student in the Master of International Studies programme , University of Otago .
