Given New Zealand 's comparatively limited resources , it simply makes no sense to continue to spend billions on combat equipment and military activities every year . <p> Fisheries and resource protection , border control , and maritime search and rescue could be better done by a civilian coastguard with inshore and offshore capabilities , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that are suitable for our coastline , Antarctica and the Pacific , which -- along with equipping civilian agencies for land-based search and rescue , and for humanitarian assistance here and overseas -- would be a much cheaper option as none of these require expensive combat hardware . 2 <p> That would also surely be a more useful contribution than New Zealand continuing to be involved in destructive full-scale combat assaults on land and sea through day-to-day military training , and military ' exercises ' such as RIMPAC that are not only costly , but also harmful to the environment , marine and other life , while increasing New Zealand 's climate-destroying emissions and wasting non-renewable resources . <p> New Zealand could -- and should -- be leading the way to a positive future . Instead of continuing down the path of spending billions every year and allocating billions more over the next few years for " enhanced lethality " -- including upgraded frigates , aircraft , missiles , vehicles , and drones -- this is an opportune time to choose a new and better way forward . @@137711364 <p> New Zealand 's tendency to speak softly when it comes to China is " increasingly ineffective and may become counterproductive " , a Defence Force officer argues in a new article . <p> In a piece for the Royal New Zealand Navy 's professional journal , Army Major Andrew Gifford pushes for a clearer strategy to deal with Beijing . He says an analysis of China 's actions reveals a long-term strategy " that blends influence , inducement and coercion " . <p> " Beijing is undermining the norms that underpin Pacific island stability and sovereignty . These dynamics risk marginalising Wellington 's influence and constraining its ability to act as the trusted and values-based partner of choice . " <h> read more <p> It comes as the Government announces plans to invest in uncrewed systems and surveillance vessels as part of the Budget , hoping to make better use of new technologies to project force . <p> But Gifford writes that a Taiwan confrontation or other clash in the Pacific could pull New Zealand into a conflict for which it is " militarily ill-prepared " , suggesting a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> New Zealand risks being cut out of regional decision-making if its Pacific partners are captured or coerced by Beijing , he says , adding that our country 's own over-reliance on Chinese trade and investment " constrains NZ 's policy freedom and erodes its credibility as an advocate for transparency and sovereignty " . <p> " Without a clear strategy , Wellington may find itself reacting to crises rather than shaping outcomes . To avoid this , New Zealand requires an integrated approach built on foresight and candid analysis of its strategic situation . " <p> Among the options policymakers could consider , he says New Zealand could embed transparency and parliamentary oversight into aid and infrastructure agreements , funding civil society watchdogs in the Pacific along with media and parliamentary training . <p> The country could also speed up the delivery of its aid projects , and increase climate adaptation funding to show it was in line with the Pacific 's priorities . <p> Back us when it really matters <p> Donate or subscribe today to support local independent journalism . <p> For a limited time , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ saving $91 ) and get 12 months access ( for two users ) . <p> " The perception that New Zealand delivers aid slowly , or conditionally , risks ceding influence to faster-moving competitors such as China , " Gifford says , noting that our alignment with the United States in the region could also complicate Pacific relations because of the Trump Administration 's climate change denial and reduced USAID funding . <p> Although a planned increase in defence spending is positive , " investment should be based on the threats New Zealand faces and the environment in which its forces operate , not simply to keep an atrophied limb of national power alive " . <p> The Defence Force could prioritise lower-cost , multi-role ships and platforms that were better suited to the Pacific , designed to handle small islands with shallow water , short runways and unsealed roads , rather than trying to copy larger partners ' capabilities . <p> The Ukraine war also shows the importance of investing in unmanned and precision systems , Gifford says . <p> " New Zealand is currently weak in remote and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on modernising legacy capabilities . This is the moment to deliberately plan and equip for future conflicts , not past ones . " <p> New Zealand could not and should not disengage from relations with China , which remain integral to the country 's prosperity , but has to remain alert to Beijing 's strategic intentions and how that could undermine national interests . <p> " New Zealand 's reticence in calling out China when its policies conflict with our values has reflected too much courtesy , caution , and conformity . <p> " Our diplomatic instinct to show respect and ' speak softly ' so as not to provoke Beijing is increasingly ineffective , and may become counterproductive .
